| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Joe_Biden_series)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Joe_Biden_series)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Joe_Biden_series) |

**Joseph Robinette Biden Jr.**[^cite_note-2] (born November 20, 1942) is an American retired politician who served as the 46th [president of the United States](24113.md) from 2021 to 2025. A member of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md), he [represented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Senate_career_of_Joe_Biden) [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware) in the [United States Senate](24909346.md) from 1973 to 2009 and also served as the 47th [vice president](32759.md) under President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) from 2009 to 2017.

Born in [Scranton, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scranton,_Pennsylvania), Biden graduated from the [University of Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Delaware) in 1965 and the [Syracuse University College of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_University_College_of_Law) in 1968. He was elected to the [New Castle County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Castle_County_Council) in 1970 and the [U.S. Senate in 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware). [As a senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Senate_career_of_Joe_Biden), Biden chaired the Senate [Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) and [Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Foreign_Relations). He drafted and led passage of the [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act) and the [Violence Against Women Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_Against_Women_Act). Biden oversaw six [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) confirmation hearings, including contentious [hearings for Robert Bork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork_Supreme_Court_nomination) and [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas_Supreme_Court_nomination). He opposed the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1991 but voted in favor of the [Iraq War Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_Resolution) in 2002. Biden ran unsuccessfully for the Democratic presidential nomination in the [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) and [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) primaries. In 2008, Obama chose him as his running mate, and he served as a close advisor to Obama [while in office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden). In the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md), Biden chose [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) as his running mate, and they defeated [Republican](32070.md) incumbents [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence).

As president, Biden signed the [American Rescue Plan Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021) in response to the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md) and [recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession). He signed bipartisan bills on [infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act) and [manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CHIPS_and_Science_Act). Biden proposed the [Build Back Better Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Act), part of which was incorporated into the [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act) passed in 2022. He [appointed Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson_Supreme_Court_nomination) to the [Supreme Court of the United States](31737.md). In [his foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Joe_Biden_administration), the U.S. reentered the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md) and enacted the [New Atlantic Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Atlantic_Charter). Biden [withdrew U.S. troops](66883576.md) from [Afghanistan](19666611.md) in a chaotic exit, after which the [Taliban swiftly retook control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive). He [responded](70376708.md) to the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](70149799.md) by imposing [sanctions on Russia](70323386.md) and authorizing [Ukrainian aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War). In 2022, Biden supported [Finland](10577.md)'s and [Sweden](5058739.md)'s bids to join [NATO](21133.md) and formally approved their membership. During the [Gaza war](74998519.md), he condemned the actions of [Hamas](13913.md), [gave Israel strong military and diplomatic support](65284610.md), sent humanitarian aid to the [Gaza Strip](12047.md), and backed [a temporary ceasefire proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) before his presidency ended.

[Concerns about Biden's age and health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden) persisted throughout his presidency. He is the first president to turn 80 years old while in office. Biden initially [ran for reelection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign) in [2024](59568546.md), winning the [Democratic primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) and becoming the party's presumptive nominee. After his performance in the [first presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate), intensifying scrutiny from both political parties about his age and health led him to [withdraw his candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election). Biden entered office with majority support, but his approval ratings declined significantly during his presidency, particularly over concerns about [inflation](69605317.md) and [immigration](5044573.md). He is the [oldest living former U.S. president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age#Age_of_presidents) since the [second inauguration of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump) in 2025, the [oldest living former U.S. vice president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_vice_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age) since [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) died in 2025, and the oldest person to have served as president.

## Early life and education

Main article: [Early life and career of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_life_and_career_of_Joe_Biden)



Biden in 1952; at age 10

Joseph Robinette Biden Jr. was born on November 20, 1942,[^cite_note-cong-bio-3] at St. Mary's Hospital in [Scranton, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scranton,_Pennsylvania),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover20105-4] to Catherine Eugenia "Jean" Biden ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names) Finnegan)[^cite_note-5] and Joseph Robinette Biden Sr.[^cite_note-6] He is the oldest child in a [Catholic](606848.md) family of predominantly [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) descent. Biden has a sister, [Valerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerie_Biden_Owens), and two brothers, [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Biden) and Francis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover20108–9-7]

Joseph Sr. had been wealthy, and the family purchased a home in the affluent Long Island suburb of [Garden City, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_City,_New_York), in 1946.[^cite_note-8] After he suffered business setbacks around the time Biden was seven years old,[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-promises2008-10][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover20107–8-11] the family lived with Jean's parents in Scranton for several years.[^cite_note-nyt-father-12] Scranton fell into economic decline during the 1950s, and Joseph Sr. could not find steady work.[^cite_note-Chogath_Kog'sante-13] Beginning in 1953, when Biden was ten,[^cite_note-14] the family lived in an apartment in [Claymont, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claymont,_Delaware), before moving to a house in nearby [Mayfield, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayfield,_Delaware).[^cite_note-17] Joseph Sr. later became a successful [used-car salesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Used-car_salesman), maintaining the family in a middle-class lifestyle.[^cite_note-nyt-father-12][^cite_note-Chogath_Kog'sante-13][^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] Growing up, Biden observed [alcoholism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholism) in his family, deciding at a young age to be [teetotal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teetotalism).[^cite_note-nyt-father-12]

At [Archmere Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archmere_Academy) in Claymont,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201027,_32-19] Biden played baseball and was a standout [halfback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halfback_\(American_football\)) and [wide receiver](492476.md) on the [high school football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_football) team.[^cite_note-nyt-father-12][^cite_note-20] Though a poor student, he was [class president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_president) in his junior and senior years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201040–41-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor199099-22] He graduated in 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201040–41-21] At the [University of Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Delaware) in [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_Delaware), Biden briefly played freshman football[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24] and received a Bachelor of Arts degree with a [double major](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_major) in history and [political science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science) in 1965.[^cite_note-ap-timeline-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor199098-26] To overcome a childhood stutter, he memorized lines from [Ralph Waldo Emerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Waldo_Emerson) and [William Butler Yeats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Butler_Yeats).[^cite_note-27]

## Law school and early career (1966–1973)

Main article: [Early career of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_life_and_career_of_Joe_Biden)

Biden earned a [Juris Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juris_Doctor) from [Syracuse University College of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_University_College_of_Law) in 1968. In his first year of law school, he failed a course because he [plagiarized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plagiarism) a law review article, but the failing grade was later stricken. His grades were relatively poor, and he graduated 76th in a class of 85.[^cite_note-nyt091887-28] He was [admitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_bar_in_the_United_States) to the [Delaware bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_bar) in 1969.[^cite_note-cong-bio-3]

Biden clerked at a law firm headed by prominent local [Republican](32070.md) William Prickett in 1968 and self-identified as a Republican.[^cite_note-dmn-87-29][^cite_note-30] He disliked incumbent [Democratic](5043544.md) Delaware governor [Charles L. Terry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_L._Terry)'s conservative racial politics and supported a more liberal Republican, [Russell W. Peterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_W._Peterson), who defeated Terry in 1968.[^cite_note-dmn-87-29] Local Republicans attempted to recruit Biden, but he registered as an [independent](2172378.md) because of his distaste for Republican presidential candidate [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon).[^cite_note-dmn-87-29]

### Law practices



Biden pictured by [Syracuse University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_University) in 1968

In 1969, Biden resumed practicing law, first as a [public defender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_defender) in [Wilmington, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_Delaware). Most of his clients were [African Americans](2154.md) from Wilmington's east side.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32] Biden then joined a firm headed by Sid Balick, a locally active Democrat.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoritz198743-33][^cite_note-dmn-87-29] Balick named him to the Democratic Forum, a group trying to reform and revitalize the state party,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201086-34] and Biden switched his registration to Democratic.[^cite_note-dmn-87-29] He also started his own firm, Biden and Walsh.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoritz198743-33] [Corporate law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_law), however, did not appeal to him, and [criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_law) did not pay well.[^cite_note-nyt-father-12] He supplemented his income by managing properties.[^cite_note-watn020109-35]

Biden ran for the fourth district seat on the [New Castle County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Castle_County,_Delaware#County_Council) in 1970 on a liberal platform that included support for public housing in the suburbs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201059-36][^cite_note-37] Biden won the general election, defeating Republican Lawrence T. Messick, and took office on January 5, 1971.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] He served until January 1, 1973.[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201052–64-41] During his time on the county council, Biden opposed large highway projects, which he argued might disrupt Wilmington neighborhoods.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201052–64-41]

Biden had not openly supported or opposed the [Vietnam War](32611.md) until he ran for Senate and opposed [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s conduct of the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201050,_75-42] While studying at the University of Delaware and Syracuse University, Biden obtained five student [draft](5735.md) deferments. Based on a physical examination, he was given a conditional medical deferment in 1968; in 2008, a spokesperson for Biden said his having had "[asthma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asthma) as a teenager" was the reason.[^cite_note-43]

### 1972 U.S. Senate campaign in Delaware

Main article: [1972 United States Senate election in Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware)

Biden defeated Republican incumbent [J. Caleb Boggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Caleb_Boggs) to become the junior U.S. senator from Delaware in 1972. He was the only Democrat willing to challenge Boggs and, with minimal campaign funds, was thought to have no chance of winning.[^cite_note-cby-43-44][^cite_note-nyt-father-12] Family members managed and staffed the campaign, which relied on meeting voters face-to-face and hand-distributing position papers,[^cite_note-npr-72-45] an approach made feasible by Delaware's small size.[^cite_note-watn020109-35] He received help from the [AFL-CIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFL-CIO) and Democratic pollster [Patrick Caddell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Caddell).[^cite_note-cby-43-44] His platform focused on the environment, withdrawal from Vietnam, civil rights, mass transit, equitable taxation, health care and public dissatisfaction with "politics as usual".[^cite_note-cby-43-44][^cite_note-npr-72-45] A few months before the election, Biden trailed Boggs by almost thirty percentage points,[^cite_note-cby-43-44] but his energy, young family, and ability to connect with voters' emotions worked to his advantage,[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] and he won with 50.5% of the vote.[^cite_note-npr-72-45]

### Teaching

From 1991 to 2008, as an [adjunct professor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjunct_professor), Biden co-taught a [seminar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminar) on [constitutional law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_law) at [Widener University School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widener_University_School_of_Law).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47]

## U.S. Senate (1973–2009)

Main article: [US Senate career of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Senate_career_of_Joe_Biden)

Elected to the U.S. Senate in [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), Biden was reelected in [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), [1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), and [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware), regularly receiving about 60% of the vote.[^cite_note-aap08-366-48] Aged 30 when first elected, he was the [seventh-youngest senator in U.S. history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_youngest_members_of_the_United_States_Congress#List_of_youngest_U.S._senators).[^cite_note-49] He was junior senator to [William Roth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Roth) until Roth was defeated in 2000.[^cite_note-50] He remains one of the [longest-serving senators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_members_of_the_United_States_Congress_by_longevity_of_service) in U.S. history.[^cite_note-51] For 36 years, he commuted from Washington to Wilmington via [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), earning him the nickname "Amtrak Joe".[^cite_note-Amtrak-1]

### Senate activities



Secretary of the Senate [Francis R. Valeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_R._Valeo) swearing in Biden into the Senate on January 5, 1973

During his early years in the Senate, Biden focused on consumer protection and environmental issues and called for greater government accountability.[^cite_note-52] In 1974, he described himself as liberal on civil rights and liberties, senior citizens' concerns, and healthcare, but conservative on other issues, including abortion and [military conscription](5735.md).[^cite_note-53] Biden was the first U.S. senator to endorse Governor [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) for president in the [1976 Democratic primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries). Carter won the Democratic nomination and the [1976 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-54] Biden also worked on [arms control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_control).[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55][^cite_note-56] After Congress failed to ratify the [SALT II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SALT_II) Treaty signed in 1979 by [Soviet general secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_general_secretary) [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) and President Carter, Biden met with Soviet foreign minister [Andrei Gromyko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Gromyko) and secured changes that addressed the Senate Foreign Relations Committee's objections.[^cite_note-57] He received considerable attention when he excoriated Secretary of State [George Shultz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Shultz) at a Senate hearing for the Reagan administration's support of [South Africa](17416221.md) despite its policy of [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid).[^cite_note-dmn-87-29] In a congressional hearing in 1984, he objected to the [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative) plan to construct autonomous systems of ICBM defense.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] Biden was an advocate for Delaware military installations, including [Dover Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dover_Air_Force_Base) and [New Castle Air National Guard Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Castle_Air_National_Guard_Base).[^cite_note-60]

In the mid-1970s, Biden was one of the Senate's strongest opponents of [race-integration busing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race-integration_busing). His Delaware constituents strongly opposed it, and such opposition nationwide later led his party to mostly abandon school integration policies.[^cite_note-Gadsden-61] In his first Senate campaign, Biden had expressed support for busing to remedy *de jure* segregation, as in the South, but opposed its use to remedy *de facto* segregation arising from racial patterns of neighborhood residency, as in Delaware; he opposed a proposed constitutional amendment banning busing entirely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGadsden2012214-62] Biden supported a 1976 measure forbidding the use of federal funds for transporting students beyond the school closest to them.[^cite_note-Gadsden-61] He co-sponsored a 1977 amendment closing loopholes in that measure, which President Carter signed into law in 1978.[^cite_note-63]

Biden became [ranking minority member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranking_minority_member) of the [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Judiciary_Committee) in 1981. He was a Democratic floor manager for the successful passage of the [Comprehensive Crime Control Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Crime_Control_Act) in 1984. His supporters praised him for modifying some of the law's worst provisions, and it was his most important legislative accomplishment to that time.[^cite_note-cby-44-64] In 1994, Biden helped pass the [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act), which included [a ban on assault weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assault_Weapons_Ban),[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] and the [Violence Against Women Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_Against_Women_Act),[^cite_note-67] which he has called his most significant legislation.[^cite_note-68] The 1994 crime law was unpopular among progressives and criticized for resulting in mass incarceration;[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] Biden later expressed regret for passing the bill.[^cite_note-71]

Biden voted for a 1993 provision that deemed homosexuality incompatible with military life, thereby banning [gay people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_gay,_lesbian_or_bisexual_people) from serving in the armed forces.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] In 1996, he voted for the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), which prohibited the federal government from recognizing same-sex marriages, thereby barring people in such marriages from equal protection under federal law and allowing states to do the same.[^cite_note-NYT_Biden_Evolution_on_LGBTQ-74] In 2015, the act was ruled unconstitutional in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*.[^cite_note-75]

Biden was critical of [Independent Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Office_of_the_Independent_Counsel) [Ken Starr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Starr) during the 1990s [Whitewater controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewater_controversy) and [Clinton–Lewinsky scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton%E2%80%93Lewinsky_scandal) investigations, saying "it's going to be a cold day in hell" before another independent counsel would be granted similar powers.[^cite_note-76] He voted to acquit during the [impeachment of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Bill_Clinton).[^cite_note-77] During the 2000s, Biden sponsored bankruptcy legislation sought by credit card issuers (such as [MBNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MBNA), one of Delaware's largest companies).[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] [Bill Clinton](3356.md) vetoed the bill in 2000, but it passed in 2005 as the [Bankruptcy Abuse Prevention and Consumer Protection Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankruptcy_Abuse_Prevention_and_Consumer_Protection_Act),[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] with Biden being one of only 18 Democrats to vote for it, while leading Democrats and consumer rights organizations opposed it.[^cite_note-79] As a senator, Biden strongly supported increased [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) funding and rail security.[^cite_note-aap08-366-48][^cite_note-80]

### Brain surgeries

In February 1988, after several episodes of severe neck pain, Biden underwent surgery to correct a leaking [intracranial berry aneurysm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intracranial_berry_aneurysm).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-nyt-4cmed-82] While recuperating, he suffered a [pulmonary embolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulmonary_embolism).[^cite_note-nyt-4cmed-82] A second aneurysm was surgically repaired in May.[^cite_note-nyt-4cmed-82][^cite_note-83] His recuperation kept him away from the Senate for seven months.[^cite_note-84]

### Senate Judiciary Committee

Photo of Biden shaking hands with Reagan in the Oval Office

Biden shaking hands with President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in 1984

Biden was a longtime member of the [Senate Committee on the Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary). He chaired it from 1987 to 1995 and was a [ranking minority member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranking_member) from 1981 to 1987 and again from 1995 to 1997.[^cite_note-85]

As chair, Biden presided over two highly contentious [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md) confirmation hearings.[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] When [Robert Bork was nominated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork_Supreme_Court_nomination) in 1988, Biden reversed his approval‍—‌given in an interview the previous year‍—‌of a hypothetical Bork nomination. Conservatives were angered,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronner1989138–139,_214,_305-86] but at the hearings' close Biden was praised for his fairness, humor, and courage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronner1989138–139,_214,_305-86][^cite_note-nyt-lg-87-87] Rejecting the arguments of some Bork opponents,[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] Biden framed his objections to Bork in terms of the conflict between Bork's strong [originalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Originalism) and the view that the [U.S. Constitution](31644.md) provides rights to liberty and privacy beyond those explicitly enumerated in its text.[^cite_note-nyt-lg-87-87] Bork's nomination was rejected in the committee by a 5–9 vote[^cite_note-nyt-lg-87-87] and then in the full Senate, 42–58.[^cite_note-88]

During [Clarence Thomas's nomination hearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas_Supreme_Court_nomination) in 1991, Biden's questions on constitutional issues were often convoluted to the point that Thomas sometimes lost track of them,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayerAbramson1994213,_218,_336-89] and Thomas later wrote that Biden's questions were akin to "[beanballs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beanball)".[^cite_note-90] After the committee hearing closed, the public learned that [Anita Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Hill) had accused Thomas of [making unwelcome sexual comments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment) when they had worked together.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] Biden had known of some of these charges, but initially shared them only with the committee because Hill was then unwilling to testify.[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] The committee hearing was reopened and Hill testified, but Biden did not permit testimony from other witnesses, such as a woman who had made similar charges and experts on harassment.[^cite_note-nyt-hill-93] The full Senate confirmed Thomas by a 52–48 vote, with Biden opposed.[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] Liberal legal advocates and women's groups felt strongly that Biden had mishandled the hearings and not done enough to support Hill.[^cite_note-nyt-hill-93] In 2019, he told Hill he regretted his treatment of her, but Hill said afterward she remained unsatisfied.[^cite_note-94]

### Senate Foreign Relations Committee

Biden was a longtime member of the [Senate Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Foreign_Relations). He became its ranking minority member in 1997, and chaired it from June 2001 to 2003 and 2007 to 2009.[^cite_note-aap08-365-95] His positions were generally [liberal internationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_internationalism).[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55][^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] He collaborated effectively with Republicans and sometimes went against elements of his own party.[^cite_note-aap08-365-95][^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] During this time he met with at least 150 leaders from 60 countries and international organizations, becoming a well-known Democratic voice on foreign policy.[^cite_note-97]

Biden voted against authorization for the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1991.[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] He became interested in the [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars) after hearing about Serbian abuses during the [Croatian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence) in 1991.[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55] Once the [Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War) broke out, Biden was among the first to call for the "[lift and strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_and_strike_\(Bosnian_War\))" policy.[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55][^cite_note-aap08-365-95] [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) and [Bill Clinton](3356.md) were both reluctant to implement the policy, fearing Balkan entanglement.[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55][^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] In April 1993, Biden had a tense three-hour meeting with Serbian leader [Slobodan Milošević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87).[^cite_note-wapo-bosnia-98] Biden worked on several versions of legislative language urging the U.S. toward greater involvement.[^cite_note-wapo-bosnia-98] He has called his role in affecting Balkan policy in the mid-1990s his "proudest moment in public life" related to foreign policy.[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] In 1999, during the [Kosovo War](16760.md), Biden supported the [NATO bombing of Yugoslavia](322473.md).[^cite_note-nyt-foreign-55] He and Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) co-sponsored the McCain-Biden Kosovo Resolution, which called on Clinton to use all necessary force, including ground troops, to confront Milošević over Yugoslav actions toward [Kosovo Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_Albanians).[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96][^cite_note-wsj082508-99]

#### Wars in Afghanistan and Iraq

Main article: [War on terror](13425800.md)

Biden was a strong supporter of the [War in Afghanistan](19666611.md), saying, "Whatever it takes, we should do it."[^cite_note-100] As head of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, he said in 2002 that Iraqi president [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) was a threat to national security and there was no other option than to "eliminate" that threat.[^cite_note-101] In October 2002, he voted in favor of the [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Iraq), approving the [U.S. invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._invasion_of_Iraq).[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96] As chair of the committee, he assembled witnesses to testify in favor of the authorization. They gave testimony grossly misrepresenting the intent, history, and status of Saddam and his government, and touted Iraq's fictional possession of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction).[^cite_note-102] Biden eventually became a critic of the war, calling his vote a "mistake" by 2005,[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] but did not push for withdrawal.[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96][^cite_note-wapo-bosnia-98] He supported the appropriations for the occupation, but argued that the war should be internationalized, that more soldiers were needed, and that the Bush administration should "level with the [American people](19792942.md)" about its cost and length.[^cite_note-aap08-365-95][^cite_note-wsj082508-99]

By late 2006, Biden's stance had shifted considerably. He opposed the [troop surge of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troop_surge_of_2007),[^cite_note-lat-foreign-96][^cite_note-wapo-bosnia-98] saying General [David Petraeus](3223944.md) was "dead, flat wrong" in believing the surge could work.[^cite_note-nytm-traub-105] Biden, through a plan developed with [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations) president [Leslie H. Gelb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leslie_H._Gelb), instead advocated dividing Iraq into a loose [federation of three ethnic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_in_Iraq).[^cite_note-110] In September 2007, a non-binding resolution endorsing the plan passed the Senate,[^cite_note-111] but the idea failed to gain traction.[^cite_note-nytm-traub-105]

## Previous presidential campaigns

### 1988 campaign

Main article: [Joe Biden 1988 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_1988_presidential_campaign)



Biden speaks at a campaign event in 1987.

Biden declared his candidacy for the [1988 Democratic presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) on June 9, 1987.[^cite_note-112] He was considered a strong candidate because of his moderate image, his speaking ability, his high profile as chair of the Senate Judiciary Committee at the upcoming [Robert Bork Supreme Court nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork_Supreme_Court_nomination) hearings, and his appeal to [Baby Boomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Boomer).[^cite_note-dmn-87-29][^cite_note-nyt083187-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor199083-114] He raised more in the first quarter of 1987 than any other candidate.[^cite_note-nyt083187-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor199083-114]

By August, Biden's campaign messaging had become confused due to staff rivalries,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1990108–109-115] and in September, he was accused of plagiarizing a speech by [British Labour Party](19279158.md) leader [Neil Kinnock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Kinnock).[^cite_note-116] Biden had credited Kinnock on previous occasions,[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-lat-speech-118] but did not on two occasions in August.[^cite_note-119]<sup>: 230–232 </sup>[^cite_note-lat-speech-118] Earlier that year, Biden had also used passages from a speech by [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) (for which his aides took blame) and the [inaugural address of John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inaugural_address_of_John_F._Kennedy). Two years earlier he had used a 1976 passage by [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey).[^cite_note-120] Biden responded that politicians often borrow from one another without giving credit, and that one of his rivals for the nomination, [Jesse Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Jackson), had called him to point out that Jackson had used the same material by Humphrey that Biden had used.[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18][^cite_note-nyt091887-28] A few days later, it was publicized that, while in law school, Biden had taken text from a *[Fordham Law Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordham_Law_Review)* article with inadequate citations.[^cite_note-nyt091887-28] At Biden's request the Delaware Supreme Court's Board of Professional Responsibility reviewed the incident and concluded that he had violated no rules.[^cite_note-121]

Biden made several false or exaggerated claims about his early life: that he had earned three degrees in college, that he attended [law school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_school) on a full scholarship, that he had graduated in the top half of his class,[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] and that he had marched in the [civil rights movement](49001.md).[^cite_note-124] The limited amount of other news about the presidential race amplified these disclosures,[^cite_note-125] and on September 23, 1987, Biden withdrew his candidacy.[^cite_note-126]

### 2008 campaign

Main article: [Joe Biden 2008 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2008_presidential_campaign)

After exploring running in several previous cycles, in January 2007, Biden declared [his candidacy in the 2008 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-aap08-366-48][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128] Biden focused on the [Iraq War](5043324.md), his record as chairman of major Senate committees, and his foreign-policy experience.[^cite_note-129] Biden was noted for his [one-liners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-line_joke) during the campaign; in one debate he said of Republican candidate [Rudy Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani), "There's only three things he mentions in a sentence: a noun, and a verb and [9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_influence_of_the_September_11_attacks)."[^cite_note-130]

Biden had difficulty raising funds, struggled to draw people to his rallies, and failed to gain traction against the high-profile candidacies of [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) and [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton).[^cite_note-131] He never rose above single digits in [national polls of the Democratic candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationwide_opinion_polling_for_the_Democratic_Party_2008_presidential_candidates). In the first contest on January 3, 2008, Biden placed fifth in the [Iowa caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa_Democratic_caucuses,_2008), garnering slightly less than one percent of the state delegates.[^cite_note-132] He withdrew from the race that evening.[^cite_note-133]

Despite its lack of success, Biden's 2008 campaign raised his stature in the political world.[^cite_note-gamechange-134]<sup>: 336 </sup> In particular, it changed the relationship between Biden and Obama. Although they had served together on the [Senate Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Foreign_Relations_Committee), they had not been close: Biden resented Obama's quick rise to political stardom,[^cite_note-nytm-traub-105][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolffe2009218-135] while Obama viewed Biden as garrulous and patronizing.[^cite_note-gamechange-134]<sup>: 28, 337–338 </sup> Having gotten to know each other during 2007, Obama appreciated Biden's campaign style and appeal to working-class voters, and Biden said he became convinced Obama was "the real deal".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolffe2009218-135][^cite_note-gamechange-134]<sup>: 28, 337–338 </sup>

## Vice presidential campaigns

### 2008 campaign

Main articles: [Barack Obama 2008 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_2008_presidential_campaign) and [2008 Democratic Party vice presidential candidate selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection)

Photo of Biden outdoors behind a lectern, with Obama seated behind him and smiling

Biden speaks at his vice presidential announcement at the [Old State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_State_Capitol_State_Historic_Site) in [Springfield, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois), on August 23, 2008.

In August 2008, Obama and Biden met in secret to discuss the possibility of a place for Biden in the Obama administration,[^cite_note-ny-lizza-136] and developed a strong personal rapport.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolffe2009218-135] On August 22, Obama announced that Biden would be his [running mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_mate).[^cite_note-137] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that the choice reflected a desire for someone with [foreign policy](233404.md) and [national security](240468.md) experience.[^cite_note-138] Others pointed out Biden's appeal to middle-class and [blue-collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-collar) voters.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolffe2009217-140] Biden was officially nominated for vice president on August 27 at the [2008 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_National_Convention) in [Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver).[^cite_note-141]

Biden's vice-presidential campaigning gained little media attention, as the press devoted far more coverage to the Republican nominee and then-[governor of Alaska](26555302.md), [Sarah Palin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Palin).[^cite_note-nyt-no2-142][^cite_note-143] Under instructions from the campaign, Biden kept his speeches succinct and tried to avoid offhand remarks.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] Privately, Biden's remarks frustrated Obama. "How many times is Biden gonna say something stupid?", he once angrily asked.[^cite_note-gamechange-134]<sup>: 411–414, 419 </sup> Obama campaign staffers called Biden's blunders "Joe bombs" and kept Biden uninformed about strategy discussions, which irked Biden.[^cite_note-nyt0508122-146] Relations between the two campaigns became strained for a month, until Biden apologized to Obama and the two built a stronger partnership.[^cite_note-gamechange-134]<sup>: 411–414 </sup>

As the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) reached a peak in September 2008, and the proposed [Emergency Economic Stabilization Act of 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Economic_Stabilization_Act_of_2008) became a major factor in the campaign, Biden voted for the \$700 billion Emergency Economic Stabilization Act of 2008, which passed in the Senate.[^cite_note-147] On October 2, he participated in the [vice-presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_debates) with Palin at [Washington University in St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_University_in_St._Louis). Post-debate polls found that while Palin exceeded many voters' expectations, Biden had still won the debate overall.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover2010655–661-148] On November 4, Obama and Biden [were elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151]

As Biden was running for vice president, he was also running for reelection to the Senate,[^cite_note-152] as permitted by Delaware law.[^cite_note-aap08-366-48] Having been [reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_Senate_election_in_Delaware) to the Senate as well as the vice presidency,[^cite_note-153] Biden made a point of not resigning from the Senate before he was sworn in for his seventh term in January 2009.[^cite_note-154] He [resigned from the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resignation_from_the_United_States_Senate) on January 15.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]

### 2012 campaign

Main article: [Barack Obama 2012 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_2012_presidential_campaign)

In October 2010, Biden said Obama had asked him to remain as his running mate for the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election),[^cite_note-nyt1013102-157] but with Obama's popularity declining, [White House Chief of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Chief_of_Staff) [William M. Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_M._Daley) conducted some secret polling and focus group research in late 2011 on the idea of replacing Biden with Hillary Clinton.[^cite_note-nyt-dd-replace-158] The notion was dropped when the results showed no appreciable improvement,[^cite_note-nyt-dd-replace-158] and White House officials later said Obama himself never entertained the idea.[^cite_note-159]

Biden's May 2012 statement that he was "absolutely comfortable" with [same-sex marriage](92656.md) gained considerable public attention in comparison to Obama's position, which had been described as "evolving".[^cite_note-160] Biden made his statement without administration consent, and Obama and his aides were irked, since Obama had planned to shift position in the build-up to the party convention.[^cite_note-nyt0508122-146][^cite_note-ap051012-161][^cite_note-162] Gay rights advocates seized upon Biden's statement,[^cite_note-ap051012-161] and within days, Obama announced that he too supported same-sex marriage, an action in part forced by Biden's remarks.[^cite_note-163]

Biden had a heavy schedule of appearances in swing states as the reelection campaign began in earnest in spring 2012.[^cite_note-time-be-joe-164][^cite_note-time-mo-165] An August 2012 remark before a mixed-race audience that Republican proposals to relax Wall Street regulations would "put y'all back in chains" again drew attention to Biden's propensity for colorful remarks.[^cite_note-time-be-joe-164][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

Following the first presidential debate of the general election, in which Obama's performance was considered surprisingly lackluster,[^cite_note-168] his lead over Romney collapsed,[^cite_note-169] putting pressure on Biden to stop the bleeding with a strong showing against the Republican vice-presidential nominee, [Paul Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ryan).[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] Some political analysts considered Biden's performance in the October 11 vice-presidential debate one of the best of his career[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] and a key factor in Obama's rebound in the polls and eventual victory.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] The debate also became memorable for the popularization of Biden's use of the phrase "a bunch of malarkey" in response to an attack by Ryan on the administration's response to the [attacks on the U.S. embassy in Benghazi](37018641.md) in September.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] Biden reused the phrase during his 2020 presidential campaign.[^cite_note-178] On November 6, Obama and Biden were reelected.[^cite_note-179]

## Vice presidency (2009–2017)

Main article: [Vice presidency of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Joe_Biden)

See also: [Presidency of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama)

### First term (2009–2013)

See also: [First inauguration of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Barack_Obama)

Photo of Biden raising his right hand, reciting the Oath

Biden being sworn in as vice president by Supreme Court Justice [John Paul Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_Stevens) on January 20, 2009

Biden said he intended to eliminate some explicit roles assumed by [George W. Bush](3414021.md)'s vice president, [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney), and did not intend to emulate any previous vice presidency.[^cite_note-180] He was sworn in on January 20, 2009.[^cite_note-181] He was the first vice president from [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware)[^cite_note-182] and the first [Roman Catholic](606848.md) vice president.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] Members of the Obama administration said Biden's role in the White House was to be a contrarian and force others to defend their positions.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2-185] White House chief of staff [Rahm Emanuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahm_Emanuel) said Biden helped counter [groupthink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupthink).[^cite_note-nyt032809-186] The Bidens maintained a relaxed atmosphere at their [official residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_One_Observatory_Circle) in Washington, often entertaining their grandchildren, and regularly returned to their home in Delaware.[^cite_note-187]

Biden oversaw [infrastructure spending from the Obama stimulus package](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Recovery_and_Reinvestment_Act_of_2009#Infrastructure_investment) intended to help counteract the [ongoing recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late-2000s_recession).[^cite_note-188] Confronted with rising unemployment through July 2009, Biden acknowledged that the administration had "misread how bad the economy was", but maintained confidence the stimulus package would create many more jobs once the pace of expenditures picked up.[^cite_note-189] When he completed that role in February 2011, he said the number of fraud incidents with stimulus monies had been less than one percent.[^cite_note-190]

Biden's off-message response to a question in April 2009, during the beginning of the [swine flu outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_flu_pandemic), led to a swift retraction by the White House.[^cite_note-191] The remark revived Biden's reputation for [gaffes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_gaffe).[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-nw-cov-1010092-193][^cite_note-194] A [hot mic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_mic) picked up Biden telling Obama that his signing the [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act) was "a big fucking deal" on March 23, 2010.[^cite_note-195] Despite their different personalities, Obama and Biden formed a friendship, partly based around Obama's daughter Sasha and Biden's granddaughter Maisy, who attended [Sidwell Friends School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidwell_Friends_School) together.[^cite_note-nyt0508122-146]

Biden visited [Kosovo](17391.md) in May 2009 and affirmed the U.S. position that its "independence is irreversible".[^cite_note-196] He lost an internal debate to Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) about [sending 21,000 new troops to Afghanistan](19666611.md),[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] but his skepticism was valued,[^cite_note-pol091609-199] and his views gained more influence as Obama reconsidered his Afghanistan strategy.[^cite_note-nw-cov-1010092-193] Obama delegated Biden to oversee Iraq policy, and he became the administration's point man in delivering messages to Iraqi leadership before the exit of U.S. troops in 2011.[^cite_note-202]

Photo of Obama, Biden, and national security staffers in the Situation Room, somberly listening to updates on the bin Laden raid

Biden, Obama and the national security team gathered in the [White House Situation Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Situation_Room) to monitor the progress of the May 2011 [mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Osama_bin_Laden) to kill [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden).

Biden campaigned heavily for Democrats in the [2010 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_elections,_2010), maintaining an attitude of optimism in the face of predictions of large-scale losses for the party.[^cite_note-nyt1013102-157] After big Republican gains in the elections and Emanuel's departure, Biden's past relationships with Republicans in Congress became more important.[^cite_note-pol1209102-203][^cite_note-nyt1211102-204] He led the successful administration effort to gain Senate approval for the [New START treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_START_treaty).[^cite_note-pol1209102-203][^cite_note-nyt1211102-204] In December 2010, Biden's advocacy for a middle ground, followed by his negotiations with Senate minority leader [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell), were instrumental in producing the administration's compromise tax package that included a temporary [extension of the Bush tax cuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extension_of_the_Bush_tax_cuts).[^cite_note-nyt1211102-204][^cite_note-205] The package passed as the [Tax Relief, Unemployment Insurance Reauthorization, and Job Creation Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Relief,_Unemployment_Insurance_Reauthorization,_and_Job_Creation_Act_of_2010).

Obama delegated Biden to lead negotiations with Congress during the [2011 U.S. debt ceiling crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_debt-ceiling_crisis_of_2011).[^cite_note-206] Biden's relationship with McConnell brought about the [Budget Control Act of 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_Control_Act_of_2011) that solved the crisis.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] Some reports suggest that Biden opposed proceeding with the May 2011 [U.S. mission to kill Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._mission_to_kill_Osama_bin_Laden),[^cite_note-time-mo-165][^cite_note-210] lest failure adversely affect Obama's reelection prospects.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] In 2012, Biden made the case for Obama's reelection: "Osama bin Laden is dead and General Motors is alive."[^cite_note-213]

Obama named Biden to head the [Gun Violence Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_Violence_Task_Force), created to address the causes of [school shootings](59628.md) and consider possible [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_control) measures in the aftermath of the [Sandy Hook Elementary School shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandy_Hook_Elementary_School_shooting), in December 2012.[^cite_note-214] Later that month, during the final days before the United States fell off the "[fiscal cliff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_fiscal_cliff)", Biden's relationship with McConnell again proved important as the two negotiated a deal that led to the [American Taxpayer Relief Act of 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Taxpayer_Relief_Act_of_2012) being passed at the start of 2013.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-ap-cliff-216]

### Second term (2013–2017)

See also: [Second inauguration of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Barack_Obama)



Biden's official portrait as vice president, 2013

Biden was inaugurated to a second term on January 20, 2013, at a small ceremony at [Number One Observatory Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_One_Observatory_Circle), his official residence, with Justice [Sonia Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor) presiding (a public ceremony took place on January 21).[^cite_note-217] He played little part in discussions that led to the October 2013 passage of the [Continuing Appropriations Act, 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuing_Appropriations_Act,_2014), which resolved the [federal government shutdown of 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_United_States_federal_government_shutdown) and the [debt-ceiling crisis of 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_debt-ceiling_crisis_of_2013). Senate Majority Leader [Harry Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Reid) and other Democratic leaders cut him out of direct talks with Congress, feeling Biden had given too much away during previous negotiations.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220]

Biden's Violence Against Women Act was reauthorized again in 2013. The act led to related developments, such as the [White House Council on Women and Girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Council_on_Women_and_Girls), begun in the first term, as well as the [White House Task Force to Protect Students from Sexual Assault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Task_Force_to_Protect_Students_from_Sexual_Assault), begun in January 2014 with Biden and [Valerie Jarrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerie_Jarrett) as co-chairs.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] He talked about sexual violence while introducing [Lady Gaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Gaga) at the [88th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/88th_Academy_Awards) in 2016, receiving a [standing ovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_ovation) from the audience.[^cite_note-223]

In 2013, Biden favored arming Syria's rebel fighters in Operation [Timber Sycamore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_Sycamore).[^cite_note-224] As the [ISIL](9087364.md) [insurgency in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013%E2%80%932017\)) intensified in 2014, renewed attention was paid to the Biden-Gelb Iraqi federalization plan of 2006, with some observers suggesting Biden had been right all along.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] He had close relationships with several Latin American leaders and visited the region 16 times during his vice presidency, the most of any president or vice president.[^cite_note-227] In August 2016, Biden visited [Serbia](29265.md), where he met with the Serbian Prime Minister [Aleksandar Vučić](13349267.md) and expressed condolences for civilian victims of the bombing campaign during the Kosovo War.[^cite_note-228] Biden never [cast a tie-breaking vote in the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_vice_presidents_of_the_United_States), making him the longest-serving vice president with this distinction.[^cite_note-229]

During his second term, Biden was often said to be preparing for a bid for the [2016 Democratic presidential nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-Itkowitz1-230] With his family, friends, and donors encouraging him in mid-2015 to enter the race, and with [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton)'s favorability ratings in decline at that time, Biden was reported to be seriously considering the prospect and a "[Draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_\(politics\)) Biden 2016" [Political action committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_action_committee) (PAC) was established.[^cite_note-Itkowitz1-230][^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] During 2015, Biden was uncertain about running, particularly due to the recent death of his son Beau,[^cite_note-233] before announcing his decision not to run that October.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236]

## Between tenures (2017–2021)



Biden attending the [state funeral of George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_George_H._W._Bush) in December 2018

Biden left office on January 20, 2017, and was succeeded by the 48th [vice president of the United States](32759.md), [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence). After leaving the vice presidency, Biden became an honorary professor at the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania), developing the [Penn Biden Center for Diplomacy and Global Engagement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Biden_Center_for_Diplomacy_and_Global_Engagement). Biden remained in that position until 2019.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238]

In 2017, Biden wrote a memoir, *[Promise Me, Dad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promise_Me,_Dad)*, and went on a book tour.[^cite_note-239] By 2019, he and his wife reported that they had earned over \$15 million since the end of his vice presidency from speaking engagements and book sales.[^cite_note-240]

Biden remained in the public eye, endorsing candidates while continuing to comment on politics, climate change, and the [presidency of Donald Trump](52231341.md).[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-foreign-policy2-242][^cite_note-243] He also continued to speak out in favor of LGBT rights, continuing advocacy on an issue he had become more closely associated with during his vice presidency.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] In 2018, he gave a eulogy for Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain).[^cite_note-246] Biden continued to support [cancer research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancer_research).[^cite_note-247]

## 2020 presidential campaign

Main article: [Joe Biden 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign)

See also: [List of Joe Biden 2020 presidential campaign endorsements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign_endorsements)

### Speculation and announcement

Photo of Biden raising his fist while standing behind a lectern

Biden at his presidential kickoff rally in [Philadelphia](50585.md), May 2019

Between 2016 and 2019, media outlets often mentioned Biden as a likely candidate for [president in 2020](48410011.md).[^cite_note-248] When asked if he would run, he gave varied and ambivalent answers, saying "never say never".[^cite_note-249] A [political action committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_action_committee) known as [Time for Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_for_Biden) was formed in January 2018.[^cite_note-250] Biden launched his campaign on April 25, 2019,[^cite_note-251] saying he was worried by the [Trump administration](52231341.md) and felt a "sense of duty".[^cite_note-252]

### Campaign

Further information: [2020 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) and [2020 United States presidential debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_debates)

Public polling showed Biden as one of the best-performing Democratic candidates head-to-head against Trump.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255] With Democrats keenly focused on "electability" for defeating Trump,[^cite_note-256] this boosted his popularity among Democratic voters.[^cite_note-257] It also made Biden a frequent target of Trump.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] In September 2019, it was reported that Trump had pressured Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) to investigate [alleged wrongdoing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden%E2%80%93Ukraine_conspiracy_theory) by Biden and his son [Hunter Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden).[^cite_note-260] No evidence was produced of any wrongdoing by the Bidens.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] Trump was perceived by many as attempting to hurt Biden's chances of winning the presidency,[^cite_note-264] resulting in [a political scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal)[^cite_note-265] and [Trump's impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-266]

In March 2019 and April 2019, eight women accused Biden of inappropriate physical contact, such as embracing, touching or kissing.[^cite_note-267] Biden had previously called himself a "tactile politician" and admitted this behavior had caused trouble for him.[^cite_note-268] Journalist [Mark Bowden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Bowden) described Biden's lifelong habit of talking close, writing that he "doesn't just meet you, he engulfs you... scooting closer" and leaning forward to talk.[^cite_note-The_Atlantic-269] In April 2019, Biden pledged to be more "respectful of people's personal space".[^cite_note-270]

Throughout 2019, Biden stayed generally ahead of other Democrats in national polls.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] Despite this, he finished fourth in the [Iowa caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Iowa_caucuses) and fifth in the [New Hampshire primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hampshire_presidential_primary).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] He performed better in the [Nevada caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevada_presidential_caucuses), reaching the 15% required for delegates, but still finished 21.6 percentage points behind [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders_2020_presidential_campaign).[^cite_note-275] Making strong appeals to Black voters on the campaign trail and in the South Carolina debate, Biden won the [South Carolina primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_South_Carolina_Democratic_primary) by more than 28 points.[^cite_note-276] After the withdrawals and subsequent endorsements of candidates [Pete Buttigieg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Buttigieg) and [Amy Klobuchar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Klobuchar), he made large gains in the [Super Tuesday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Tuesday) primaries. Biden won [18 of the next 26 contests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Results_of_the_2020_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), putting him in the lead.[^cite_note-277] [Elizabeth Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Warren) and [Michael Bloomberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Bloomberg) soon dropped out, and Biden expanded his lead with victories over Sanders in four states on March 10.[^cite_note-278] In March 2020, Biden said, "I view myself as a bridge, not as anything else. There's an entire generation of leaders you saw stand behind me. They are the future of this country."[^cite_note-279]

In late March 2020, Tara Reade, one of the eight women who in 2019 had accused Biden of inappropriate physical contact, [accused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_sexual_assault_allegation) Biden of having sexually assaulted her in 1993.[^cite_note-nytimesallegations-280] There were inconsistencies between Reade's 2019 and 2020 allegations.[^cite_note-nytimesallegations-280][^cite_note-281] Biden and his campaign denied the sexual assault allegation.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283]

When Sanders suspended his campaign on April 8, 2020, Biden became the Democratic Party's [presumptive nominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumptive_nominee).[^cite_note-284] On April 13, Sanders endorsed Biden.[^cite_note-285] [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) endorsed Biden the next day.[^cite_note-286] There was a great deal of interest in who his running mate would be, in part because of "the expectation, downplayed but not exactly denied by the Biden campaign, that the 77-year-old would be a one-term president".[^cite_note-NYT-287] Biden said, "I view myself as a transition candidate."[^cite_note-NYT-287] On August 11, Biden announced [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) as his running mate, making her the first African American and first [South Asian American](11353572.md) vice-presidential nominee on a major-party ticket.[^cite_note-288] On August 18, at the [2020 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Democratic_National_Convention), Biden officially became the Democratic Party nominee for president in the [2020 election](48410011.md).[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290]

### Presidential election and transition

Main articles: [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md) and [Presidential transition of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Joe_Biden)

Results for the 2020 United States presidential election

2020 electoral vote results. Biden won 306–232.

Biden was [elected](48410011.md) the 46th president in November 2020, defeating the incumbent, [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-291] Trump and numerous other Republicans repeatedly made [false claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump) that widespread [electoral fraud](382165.md) had occurred and that only he had legitimately won the election.[^cite_note-TrumpMakesClaimsOfElectionFraud-294] Biden's transition was delayed by several weeks as the White House ordered federal agencies not to cooperate.[^cite_note-295] On November 23, [General Services Administrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Services_Administration) [Emily W. Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_W._Murphy) formally recognized Biden as the apparent winner of the 2020 election and authorized the start of a transition process to the Biden administration.[^cite_note-296]

Although most resulting lawsuits were either dismissed or ruled against by numerous courts,[^cite_note-CourtsDismissClaimsOfElectionFraud-303] Trump nonetheless [conspired](3406102.md)[^cite_note-JackSmithReportVol1-292]<sup>: 33–53 </sup> with his [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) team to [submit documents in several states](71378172.md) (all of which Biden had won) that [falsely claimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Making_false_statements) to be legitimate electoral votes for President Trump and Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence).[^cite_note-TrumpFakeElectors-307] After the submission of these documents, the Trump campaign intended that the [presiding officer of the United States Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presiding_Officer_of_the_United_States_Senate), either [President of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Senate_of_the_United_States) Pence or [President pro tempore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_pro_tempore_of_the_United_States_Senate) [Chuck Grassley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Grassley), would claim the unilateral power to reject [electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College#Electors) during the [January 6, 2021, vote counting session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count); the presiding officer would reject all electors from the several states for which the Trump campaign had submitted false documents, leaving 232 votes for Trump and 222 votes for Biden, thereby [overturning the election results](65905202.md) in Trump's favor.[^cite_note-UnilateralRejectionOfElectors-314] This plan failed after Pence refused to cooperate with it.[^cite_note-PenceRefusesToRejectElectors-317] Trump nevertheless urged his supporters on January 6 to march to the [Capitol](31979.md) while the joint session of Congress was assembled there to count electoral votes and formalize Biden's victory, whereupon [hundreds of people stormed](66307490.md) the building and interrupted the count. During the attack, Biden addressed the nation, calling the events "an unprecedented assault unlike anything we've seen in modern times".[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] After the Capitol was cleared, Congress officially counted the election results, with Pence, in his role as president of the Senate, announcing Biden and Harris as the winners.[^cite_note-320] On January 7, Trump acknowledged the incoming administration without mentioning Biden's name.[^cite_note-321]

## Presidency (2021–2025)

Main article: [Presidency of Joe Biden](65732174.md)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Joe Biden presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Joe_Biden_presidency).

### Inauguration

Main article: [Inauguration of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden)

Photo of Biden raising his right hand, with his left hand placed on a thick Bible

Biden taking the [presidential oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_President_of_the_United_States) administered by [Chief Justice](31739.md) [John Roberts](1928850.md) at [the Capitol](31979.md) on January 20, 2021

Biden was inaugurated as the 46th president of the United States on January 20, 2021.[^cite_note-hunnicutt-322][^cite_note-323] At 78, he was then the oldest person to assume the office up to that point.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-hunnicutt-322][^cite_note-326] Biden was the second [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) U.S. president, after [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy),[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] and the first president elected from the state of [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware).[^cite_note-329] He was also the first person since [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) to have been both vice president and president,[^cite_note-330] and the only president to date from the [Silent Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_Generation).[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] Biden's inauguration was "a muted affair unlike any previous inauguration" due to COVID-19 precautions as well as massively increased security measures because of the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md).[^cite_note-333]

### First 100 days

Main article: [First 100 days of the Biden presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_the_Biden_presidency)

In his first two days as president, Biden signed 17 executive orders. By his third day, orders had included rejoining the [Paris Climate Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Climate_Agreement), ending the state of national emergency at the [border with Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border), directing the government to rejoin the [World Health Organization](33583.md), [face mask requirements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100_Days_Masking_Challenge) on [federal property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_lands), measures to combat [hunger in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-338] and revoking permits for the construction of the [Keystone XL pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_XL_pipeline).[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

On March 11, Biden signed into law the [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021), a \$1.9 trillion [economic stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(economics\)) and relief package that he had proposed to support the United States' recovery from the [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States) and health effects of the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-342] The package included direct payments to most Americans, an extension of increased unemployment benefits, funds for vaccine distribution and school reopenings, and expansions of health insurance subsidies and the [child tax credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_tax_credit_\(United_States\)). Biden's initial proposal included an increase of the [federal minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_the_United_States) to \$15 per hour, but after the [Senate parliamentarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentarian_of_the_United_States_Senate) determined that including the increase in a [budget reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_reconciliation) bill would violate Senate rules, Democrats removed it.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345]

Also in March, amid [a rise in migrants entering the U.S. from Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_crisis), Biden said migrant adults were "being sent back", in reference to the continuation of the Trump administration's Title 42 policy for quick deportations.[^cite_note-346] He earlier announced that his administration would not deport unaccompanied migrant children; the rise in arrivals of such children exceeded the capacity of facilities meant to shelter them, leading the Biden administration in March to direct the [Federal Emergency Management Agency](73797.md) to help.[^cite_note-347]

On April 14, Biden announced that the United States [would delay the withdrawal of all troops](66883576.md) from the [war in Afghanistan](19666611.md) until September 11, signaling an end to the country's direct military involvement in Afghanistan after nearly 20 years.[^cite_note-348] In February 2020, the Trump administration had [made a deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal) with the Taliban to completely withdraw U.S. forces by May 1, 2021.[^cite_note-images-349] Biden's decision met with a range of reactions, from support and relief to trepidation at the possible collapse of the Afghan government without American support.[^cite_note-350] On April 22–23, Biden held an [international climate summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Leaders'_Climate_Summit) at which he announced that the U.S. would cut its [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) by 50%–52% by 2030 compared to 2005 levels.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] On April 28, the eve of his 100th day in office, Biden delivered his [first address to a joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Joe_Biden_speech_to_a_joint_session_of_Congress).[^cite_note-353]

### Domestic policy

See also: [Social policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)

Biden delivers a [speech at a joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Joe_Biden_speech_to_a_joint_session_of_Congress) with Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and [House Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) on April 28, 2021. It was the first time a woman had occupied the Senate President chair during a joint session of Congress.

On June 17, Biden signed the [Juneteenth National Independence Day Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth_National_Independence_Day_Act), which officially declared [Juneteenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth) a [federal holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_holidays_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-354] In July 2021, amid a slowing of [the COVID-19 vaccination rate in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_States) and the spread of the [SARS-CoV-2 Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant), Biden said that it was "gigantically important" for Americans to be vaccinated.[^cite_note-355]

In 2022, Biden endorsed a change to the [Senate filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate) to allow for the passing of the [Freedom to Vote Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_to_Vote_Act) and [John Lewis Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis_Voting_Rights_Act).[^cite_note-356] The rules change failed when two Democratic senators joined Senate Republicans in opposing it.[^cite_note-357] In April 2022, Biden signed into law the bipartisan [Postal Service Reform Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_Service_Reform_Act_of_2022) to revamp the finances and operations of the [United States Postal Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service) agency.[^cite_note-358] Biden supported the [Bipartisan Safer Communities Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipartisan_Safer_Communities_Act) aimed to address gun reform issues following the [Robb Elementary School shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robb_Elementary_School_shooting) in [Uvalde, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uvalde,_Texas);[^cite_note-359] he signed the bill on June 25, 2022.[^cite_note-360]

The [Honoring our PACT Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honoring_our_PACT_Act_of_2022) was introduced in 2021 and signed into law by Biden on August 10, 2022.[^cite_note-361] The act intended to significantly improve healthcare access and funding for veterans who were exposed to toxic substances, including [burn pits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burn_pits), during military service.[^cite_note-362]

In 2022, Biden signed the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act), which repealed the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) and requires the federal government to recognize the validity of [same-sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) and [interracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage_in_the_United_States) marriages.[^cite_note-363] In June 2024, Biden issued an executive action offering amnesty to unauthorized immigrants married to American citizens. The program included a pathway to U.S. residency and citizenship and was expected to initially affect about 500,000 people. It was later struck down due to lack of legislation empowering the president to enact the program.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365]
