In January 2025, Biden declared the lapsed [Equal Rights Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Rights_Amendment) ratified as the "28th Amendment" to the [constitution](31644.md). The declaration has no formal effect[^cite_note-Stokols-366][^cite_note-367] and the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_of_the_United_States) has said it does not intend to certify the amendment as part of the constitution due to "established legal, judicial, and procedural decisions".[^cite_note-368]

#### Economic policy

Main article: [Economic policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)



Percentage of uninsured Americans under age 65 from 2010 to 2022. The number and percentage of those uninsured under Biden fell to their lowest levels since 1997.

Biden entered office nine months into a recovery from the [COVID-19 recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession) and his first year in office was characterized by robust growth in real GDP, employment, wages, and stock market returns, amid [significantly elevated inflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_inflation_surge). Real GDP grew 5.9%, the fastest rate in 37 years.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370] Amid record job creation, the unemployment rate fell at the fastest pace on record.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] By the end of 2021, inflation rates measured by the consumer price index (CPI) reached a nearly 40-year high of 7.1%, which was partially offset by the highest nominal wage and salary growth in at least 20 years. The inflation rate peaked at 9% in June 2022.[^cite_note-379] The inflation rate reached 2.9% and core inflation rate reached 3.2% on an annual basis in December 2024, the last full month of Biden's term. Between December 2020 and December 2024, CPI rose 21.3% overall, with an annualized inflation rate of 5.3% throughout Biden's term in office. The inflation rate remained above the Federal Reserve's 2% target every month since March 2021, resulting in elevated interest rates to combat inflation. Average wages increased 19% throughout Biden's presidency, falling behind inflation. The unemployment rate declined by over 2% and real GDP grew 11% during Biden's term. Total household net worth increased by 28%, largely driven by stocks and real estate. The national debt grew to \$36.2 trillion, with a debt to GDP ratio of 123% and a deficit to GDP ratio of 6% in FY 2024.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-chartingecon-374][^cite_note-381] In February 2023, the unemployment rate fell to 3.4%, a 53-year low.[^cite_note-382]

Amid a surge in [inflation](69605317.md) and [high gas prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_global_energy_crisis), Biden's approval ratings declined, with his disapproval rating surpassing his approval rating in early 2022.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384][^cite_note-385] After 5.9% growth in 2021, real GDP growth cooled in 2022 to 2.1%, after slightly negative growth in the first half spurred recession concerns. Job creation and consumer spending remained strong, as the unemployment rate fell to match a 53-year low of 3.5% in December. Inflation peaked at 9.1% in June before easing to 3.2% by October 2023. Stocks had had their worst year since 2008[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] before recovering. Widespread predictions of an imminent recession did not materialize in 2022 or 2023, and by late 2023 indicators showed sharply lower inflation with economic acceleration. GDP growth hit 4.9% in the third quarter of 2023 and the year ended with stocks near record highs, with robust holiday spending.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391]

Biden signed numerous major pieces of economic legislation in the [117th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/117th_United_States_Congress), including the [American Rescue Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021), [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act), [CHIPS and Science Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CHIPS_and_Science_Act), and the [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act).[^cite_note-392] He signed the CHIPS and Science Act into law on August 9, 2022.[^cite_note-393] It provided billions of dollars in new funding to boost domestic research on and manufacture of [semiconductors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductors), to [compete economically with China](277880.md#Economic_relations).[^cite_note-394] In his third month in office, Biden also signed an executive order to increase the minimum wage for federal contractors to \$15 per hour. The order went into effect for 390,000 workers in January 2022.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] His administration rigorously enforced [antitrust law](92025.md).

In 2022, Biden blocked a [national railroad strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_railroad_labor_dispute) planned by multiple labor unions.[^cite_note-397] During the [United Auto Workers strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Auto_Workers_strike), he expressed support for the workers.[^cite_note-398] Biden joined striking UAW workers' [picket line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picketing) in Michigan, becoming the first president to join a picket line.[^cite_note-399] He refused to block a [port strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_port_strike) from the [International Longshoremen's Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Longshoremen's_Association) in October 2024.[^cite_note-400]

Over the course of five days in March 2023, [three small- to mid-size U.S. banks failed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_banking_crisis), triggering a sharp decline in global bank stock prices and swift response by regulators to prevent potential global [contagion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_contagion). After [Silicon Valley Bank collapsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_Silicon_Valley_Bank), the first to do so, Biden expressed opposition to a bailout by taxpayers.[^cite_note-401] He claimed that the [partial rollback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Growth,_Regulatory_Relief,_and_Consumer_Protection_Act) of [Dodd-Frank regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodd%E2%80%93Frank_Wall_Street_Reform_and_Consumer_Protection_Act) contributed to the bank's failure.[^cite_note-402]

At the beginning of the [118th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/118th_United_States_Congress), Biden and congressional Republicans [engaged in a standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_States_debt-ceiling_crisis) after the U.S. hit its [debt limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_debt_ceiling), which raised the risk that the U.S. would [default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Default_\(finance\)) on its debt.[^cite_note-403] Biden and House speaker [Kevin McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_McCarthy) struck a deal to raise the debt limit, the [Fiscal Responsibility Act of 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_Responsibility_Act_of_2023), which suspended the debt limit until January 2025. Biden signed it on June 3, averting a default.[^cite_note-404] The deal was generally seen as favorable to Biden.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406]

Biden extended the COVID-19 student loan pause through September 2023, with an "on ramp" period that extended some of the pause's protections against credit reporting, collection efforts, and late payment fees through September 30, 2024.[^cite_note-407] The Biden administration's attempts to implement student loan forgiveness and relief programs have faced legal challenges from a coalition of Republican-led states.[^cite_note-408] Biden's plans to forgive student loan debt were estimated to cost over \$519 billion,[^cite_note-409] and some critics called them a "disaster".[^cite_note-410]

#### Judiciary

Further information: [List of federal judges appointed by Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Joe_Biden)

Photo of Biden and Jackson looking at an off-camera television screen

Biden and [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson) watching the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) vote on her confirmation, April 2022

By the end of 2021, 40 of his nominees to the federal judiciary had been confirmed, more than any president in his first year in office since [Ronald Reagan](25433.md).[^cite_note-411] Biden prioritized diversity in his judicial appointments more than any president in U.S. history, with most of his appointees being women and people of color.[^cite_note-412]

In January 2022, Supreme Court justice [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer) announced his intention to retire. During his 2020 campaign, Biden vowed to nominate the first Black woman to the Supreme Court if a vacancy occurred,[^cite_note-413] a promise he reiterated after Breyer announced his retirement.[^cite_note-414] On February 25, [Biden nominated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson_Supreme_Court_nomination) federal judge [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson).[^cite_note-415] She was sworn in on June 30.[^cite_note-416] By the end of his presidency, Biden had appointed 235 judges, more appointments in a single term than any other president in at least 50 years. 63% of Biden's judges were women and 60% were non-white.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] Biden expressed interest in judicial [term limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_limits_in_the_United_States#Federal_term_limits) and a binding ethics code for Supreme Court justices.[^cite_note-BidenSCOTUSreform-419]

#### Infrastructure and climate

Further information: [Build Back Better Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Plan) and [Environmental policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)

As part of Biden's Build Back Better agenda, in late March 2021, he proposed the [American Jobs Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jobs_Plan), a \$2 trillion package addressing issues including transport infrastructure, utilities infrastructure, broadband infrastructure, housing, schools, manufacturing, research and workforce development.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] After months of negotiations among Biden and lawmakers, in August 2021 the Senate passed a \$1 trillion bipartisan infrastructure bill called the [Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_Investment_and_Jobs_Act),[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] while the House, also in a bipartisan manner, approved that bill in early November 2021, covering infrastructure related to transport, utilities, and broadband.[^cite_note-424] Biden signed the bill into law in mid-November 2021.[^cite_note-425]

The other core part of the Build Back Better agenda was the [Build Back Better Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build_Back_Better_Act), a \$3.5 trillion social spending bill that expands the [social safety net](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_safety_net) and includes major provisions on climate change.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427] Democrats attempted to pass it on a [party-line vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-line_vote) through [budget reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_\(United_States_Congress\)), but struggled to win the support of Senator [Joe Manchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Manchin), even as the price was lowered to \$2.2 trillion.[^cite_note-428] After Manchin rejected the bill,[^cite_note-429] it was comprehensively reworked into the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022), covering deficit reduction, climate change, healthcare, and tax reform.[^cite_note-430]

The [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022) was introduced by Manchin and Senator [Chuck Schumer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Schumer).[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] The package aimed to raise \$739 billion and authorize \$370 billion in spending on energy and [climate change](5042951.md), \$300 billion in deficit reduction, three years of [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) subsidies, prescription drug reform to lower prices, and [tax reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_reform).[^cite_note-433] According to an analysis by the Rhodium Group, the bill will lower [U.S. greenhouse gas emissions](18246585.md) between 31 percent and 44 percent below 2005 levels by 2030.[^cite_note-Joan-434] On August 7, 2022, the Senate passed the bill (as amended) on a 51–50 vote, with all Democrats voting in favor, all Republicans opposed, and Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) [breaking the tie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_the_vice_president_of_the_United_States). Biden signed the bill on August 16.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436]

Before and during the [2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference) (COP26), Biden promoted an agreement that the U.S. and the [European Union](9317.md) cut methane emissions by a third by 2030 and tried to add dozens of other countries to the effort.[^cite_note-437] Biden pledged to double climate funding to developing countries by 2024.[^cite_note-438] Also at COP26, the U.S. and China reached a deal on greenhouse gas emission reduction. The two countries are responsible for 40 percent of global emissions.[^cite_note-439] In July 2023, when [heat waves hit the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Western_North_America_heat_wave), Biden announced measures to protect the population and said the heat waves were linked to climate change.[^cite_note-440][^cite_note-441] In April 2024, he unveiled a plan to protect and restore natural water sources (3.2 million hectares of [wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetland) and 161,000 km of waterways).[^cite_note-442]

Biden protected 674 million acres of land and ocean from natural resource exploitation, more than any other president. The vast majority of the conservation came from a ban on offshore drilling in 625 million acres of ocean.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444]

#### Immigration

Main article: [Immigration policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)



Yearly numbers of illegal immigrants apprehended at the [U.S. southern border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border)

[^cite_note-IllegalCrossings_2001--445]

On taking office, Biden unveiled the [U.S. Citizenship Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Citizenship_Act_of_2021) and moved to dismantle several policies implemented under [Donald Trump](4848272.md), halting construction of the [Mexico–United States border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall), ending Trump's [travel ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13780) on countries with predominantly Muslim populations, and signing an executive order to reaffirm protections for [DACA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DACA) recipients.[^cite_note-Narea-446][^cite_note-Bradner-447][^cite_note-\:02-448] The [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) narrowed the scope of interior immigration enforcement, directing [Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Immigration_and_Customs_Enforcement) to prioritize national security and violent crime concerns.[^cite_note-\:2-449]

[Illegal border crossings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_entry) at the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border) began to surge in 2021 when Biden assumed office,[^cite_note-454] reaching an all-time monthly high in December 2023.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456] Throughout 2024, crossings began to significantly decline from the December record, after Biden implemented restrictions on [asylum](7195971.md) claims from migrants who cross the border between ports of entry and urged Mexico to crack down on migrants.[^cite_note-cbs-border-crossings-457][^cite_note-458][^cite_note-459] Deportations from October 2023 to September 2024 reached the highest level since 2014.[^cite_note-460] Biden used [humanitarian parole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_parole) to mitigate illegal border crossings, allowing migrants to fly into the U.S. or schedule their entries through official entry points in the U.S.-Mexico border. Over a million migrants had been admitted to the U.S. under humanitarian parole as of January 2024.[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463]

Between January 2021 and January 2024, the US Border Patrol confirmed more than 7.2 million illegal migrants trying to cross the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border), not counting [gotaways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gotaways).[^cite_note-n197-464][^cite_note-i231-465][^cite_note-466] 2023 was a record year, with over 2.5 million encounters.[^cite_note-y193-467] In response to sustained high levels of migration and growing political pressure, the administration implemented stricter measures beginning in 2023. Biden faced criticism from immigration advocates for extending [Title 42](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_42_expulsion), a Trump administration border restriction that arose due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), and for restarting the use of "expedited removal" of certain Central American families.[^cite_note-Expedited-468] In May 2023, the Biden administration approved sending 1,500 [more troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Faithful_Patriot) to the U.S.-Mexico border as Title 42 expired.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470]

In January 2024, Biden expressed support for a proposed bipartisan immigration deal led by Senators [Kyrsten Sinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrsten_Sinema) and [James Lankford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Lankford).[^cite_note-471][^cite_note-472] The proposed bipartisan bill would have allowed [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) (DHS) to close the border when encounters reach a seven-day average of 5,000 or exceed 8,500 in a single day.[^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474] In addition, the bill would have mandated the detention of migrants seeking asylum and undergoing asylum interviews, with those failing the process repatriated to their home countries.[^cite_note-475] While not addressing the status of "[Dreamers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deferred_Action_for_Childhood_Arrivals)", it would have changed immigration law to allow the children of those with [H-1B visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H-1B_visas) to get work authorizations and freeze their legal ages while waiting for green cards, rather than face deportation once they turn 21, and provide additional funding for immigration judges.[^cite_note-476]

Former president Donald Trump announced his opposition to the legislation, calling on Congressional Republicans to oppose it; subsequently, leaders such as [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Mike Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Johnson) announced their opposition, halting further legislative action.[^cite_note-477][^cite_note-478] As a result of continued high immigration levels throughout his tenure, some lawmakers and pundits have criticized Biden's handling of the southern border.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480][^cite_note-481] Criticism of the bill and broader immigration policy continued to be expressed by both sides, with some liberals considering his policies too harsh while some conservatives considered them too lax.[^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] On January 17, 2024, a Republican-led non-binding resolution denouncing the Biden-Harris administration's handling of the U.S. southern border passed the House of Representatives by a vote of 225–187, with 211 Republicans and 14 Democrats supporting it.[^cite_note-484] In the final year of his presidency, the Biden administration worked to extend at least 14 contracts with private prison companies to run immigrant detention centers, despite his 2020 campaign promise to end the practice.[^cite_note-485] In June 2024, Biden issued an [executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Proclamation_on_Securing_the_Border) allowing the president to restrict the Mexico–U.S. border.[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-cbs-border-crossings-457]

#### Pardons and commutations

Biden issued more individual pardons and commutations than any other president.[^cite_note-APpardons-487] In October 2022, he pardoned all Americans convicted of "small" amounts of [cannabis](1481886.md) possession under federal law.[^cite_note-488] In December 2023, he pardoned Americans for cannabis use or possession on federal lands regardless of whether they had been charged or prosecuted.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-490] In December 2024, in the largest single-day clemency act in history, Biden granted clemency to about 1,500 nonviolent felons in [home confinement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_confinement) who had previously been released from prison.[^cite_note-491][^cite_note-492] The act generated controversy, as it included felons such as [Michael Conahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Conahan), a judge involved in the [kids for cash kickback scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kids_for_cash), and [Rita Crundwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rita_Crundwell), a comptroller responsible for the single largest municipal fraud in U.S. history. The Biden administration said the offenders who received clemency "deserve a second chance" and were selected based on meeting certain criteria in a uniform decision.[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494] Also in December 2024, Biden commuted the sentences of 37 out of 40 federal [death row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_row) inmates.[^cite_note-495] On his last full day in office, Biden commuted the sentence of [Leonard Peltier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_Peltier) to house arrest. Peltier had been convicted of murdering two FBI agents in 1975 and had served almost 50 years in prison.[^cite_note-496] Biden also pardoned two Democratic officials: Ernest William Cromartie, who had served his prison sentence for [tax evasion](2256795.md), and [Gerald G. Lundergan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_G._Lundergan), convicted of a scheme to funnel money to his daughter's Senate campaign and released in 2023.[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498][^cite_note-499]

On December 1, 2024, Biden [issued a "full and unconditional" pardon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden#Pardon_of_criminal_offenses) to his son [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden) that covered all federal offenses between that day and 2014, breaking his June 2024 pledge not to do so.[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501] In a statement announcing the pardon, Biden said he believed his son was "selectively, and unfairly, prosecuted".[^cite_note-502]

On his last full day in office, Biden pardoned five family members, including [his brother James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Biden), and others targeted for retribution by his successor.[^cite_note-politicosundaypardon-503] They included former chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff [Mark Milley](38091005.md), former [National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases](1013155.md) director [Anthony Fauci](1227250.md), and members and participants in the [House Select Committee on the January 6 Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Select_Committee_on_the_January_6_Attack).[^cite_note-politicosundaypardon-503][^cite_note-APpardons-487] Biden said the pardons were preemptive, to protect the individuals against the [incoming Trump administration's politically motivated vengeance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeting_of_political_opponents_and_civil_society_under_the_second_Trump_administration), and should not be seen as an acknowledgment of wrongdoing.[^cite_note-politicosundaypardon-503][^cite_note-bbc.com-504][^cite_note-cnn.com-505]

#### 2022 elections

Main article: [2022 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections)

On September 2, 2022, in a nationally broadcast [Philadelphia speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_for_the_Soul_of_the_Nation_speech), Biden called for a "battle for the soul of the nation". Off camera, he called Trump supporters "semi-fascists", which Republican commentators denounced.[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507][^cite_note-508] A predicted Republican [wave election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_elections_in_the_United_States) did not materialize and the race for [U.S. Congress](31756.md) control was much closer than expected, with Republicans securing a slim majority of [222 seats in the House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections),[^cite_note-513] and the Democratic caucus keeping control of the [U.S. Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_Senate_elections).[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-518]

It was the first midterm election since [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_gubernatorial_elections) in which the incumbent president's party achieved a net gain in governorships, and the first since [1934](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1934_United_States_elections) in which the president's party lost no state legislative chambers.[^cite_note-519] Democrats credited Biden for their unexpectedly strong performance,[^cite_note-520] but they likely overperformed for other reasons, including [the Supreme Court overturning *Roe v. Wade*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization) and poor Republican candidate quality in many races.[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522]

### Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign policy of the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Biden_administration)

In June 2021, Biden took his first trip abroad as president, visiting Belgium, Switzerland, and the United Kingdom. He attended [a G7 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/47th_G7_summit), [a NATO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Brussels_NATO_summit), and an EU summit, and held [one-on-one talks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Russia%E2%80%93United_States_summit) with Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-523] In September 2021, Biden announced [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS), a [defense pact](4991894.md) between Australia, the United Kingdom and the United States, to ensure "peace and stability in the Indo-Pacific".[^cite_note-524]

In February 2021, the Biden administration announced that the United States was ending its [support for the Saudi-led bombing campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._support_for_Saudi-led_operations_in_Yemen) in Yemen and revoked the designation of Yemen's [Houthis](17240332.md) as terrorists.[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-526] In early February 2022, Biden ordered the counterterrorism raid in northern Syria that resulted in the [death of Abu Ibrahim al-Hashimi al-Qurashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ibrahim_al-Hashimi_al-Qurashi#Death), the second leader of the [Islamic State](9087364.md).[^cite_note-527] In late July, Biden approved the [drone strike](38212231.md) that [killed Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Ayman_al-Zawahiri), the second leader of [Al-Qaeda](1921.md), and an integral member in the planning of the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-528] The [2022 OPEC+ oil production cut](166346.md#2022_oil_production_cut) caused a diplomatic spat with Saudi Arabia, threatening a longstanding [alliance](9753135.md).[^cite_note-529][^cite_note-530]

In August 2024, Biden negotiated and oversaw the [2024 Ankara prisoner exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Ankara_prisoner_exchange), the largest [prisoner exchange](17209653.md) since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md). It involved the release of 26 people, including journalist [Evan Gershkovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Gershkovich) and former Marine [Paul Whelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Whelan).[^cite_note-531] In November 2024, the Biden administration announced that it had helped broker a [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Israel%E2%80%93Lebanon_ceasefire_agreement) agreement in the [Israel–Hezbollah conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Hezbollah_conflict_\(2023%E2%80%932024\)).[^cite_note-536]

#### Withdrawal from Afghanistan

Main article: [2020–2021 U.S. troop withdrawal from Afghanistan](66883576.md)

Photo of Biden seated alone at a table, looking at a videoconference screen

Biden in a video conference with Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and the [U.S. National Security team](44026.md), discussing the [Fall of Kabul](68481047.md) on August 15, 2021

American forces had begun withdrawing from Afghanistan in 2020, under the provisions of a [February 2020 US-Taliban agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal) that set a May 1, 2021, deadline.[^cite_note-537] The Taliban began [an offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive) on May 1.[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539] By early July, most American troops in Afghanistan had withdrawn.[^cite_note-images-349] Biden addressed the withdrawal in July, saying, "The likelihood there's going to be the Taliban overrunning everything and owning the whole country is highly unlikely."[^cite_note-images-349]

On August 15, [the Afghan government collapsed](68481047.md) under the Taliban offensive, and Afghan President [Ashraf Ghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Ghani) fled the country.[^cite_note-images-349][^cite_note-messy-540] Biden reacted by ordering 6,000 American troops to assist with evacuating American personnel and Afghan allies.[^cite_note-541] He faced bipartisan criticism for the manner of the withdrawal,[^cite_note-542] with the evacuations described as chaotic and botched.[^cite_note-543][^cite_note-544][^cite_note-545] On August 16, Biden addressed the "messy" situation, taking responsibility for it, and admitting that the situation "unfolded more quickly than we had anticipated".[^cite_note-messy-540][^cite_note-buck1-546] He defended his decision to withdraw, saying that Americans should not be "dying in a war that Afghan forces are not willing to fight for themselves".[^cite_note-buck1-546][^cite_note-547]

On August 26, a [suicide bombing at the Kabul airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Kabul_airport_attack) killed 13 U.S. service members and 169 Afghans. On August 27, an American drone strike killed two ISIS-K targets, who were "planners and facilitators", according to a U.S. Army general.[^cite_note-548] The U.S. military completed its withdrawal from Afghanistan on August 30. Biden called the extraction of over 120,000 Americans, Afghans, and other allies "an extraordinary success".[^cite_note-549] He acknowledged that up to 200 Americans who wanted to leave did not, despite his August 18 pledge to keep troops in Afghanistan until all Americans who wanted to leave had left.[^cite_note-550]

After the withdrawal, the U.S. continued to send aid to Afghanistan, remaining its biggest aid donor as of August 2024 and spending at least \$20.7 billion post-withdrawal. U.S. funding has helped support the Taliban government and stabilize Afghanistan's economy.[^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552] On September 25, 2024, the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md) passed a resolution condemning the Biden administration for the U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan, with ten Democrats and all Republicans voting in favor.[^cite_note-553]

#### 2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine

Further information: [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and [United States and the Russo-Ukrainian war](70376708.md)

In February 2022, the [Russian Armed Forces](25709.md) under President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) launched [an invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md). After warning for several weeks that an attack was imminent, Biden responded by imposing severe [sanctions on Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) and authorizing over \$8 billion in [weapons shipments to Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War).[^cite_note-554][^cite_note-555][^cite_note-556] On April 29, he asked Congress for \$33 billion for Ukraine,[^cite_note-557][^cite_note-558] but lawmakers later increased it to about \$40 billion.[^cite_note-559][^cite_note-560][^cite_note-561] Biden blamed Putin for the emerging [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_global_energy_crisis) and [food crises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_food_crises).[^cite_note-562][^cite_note-563][^cite_note-564]

In 2022, Congress approved about \$113 billion in aid to Ukraine.[^cite_note-565] In October 2023, the Biden administration requested an additional \$61.4 billion in aid for Ukraine for the year ahead,[^cite_note-566] but delays in the passage of further aid by the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) inhibited progress, with the additional \$61 billion in aid to Ukraine added in April 2024.[^cite_note-567][^cite_note-568][^cite_note-569] Actually delivered aid often differed from announced levels and was also often delayed. The [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) and [Pentagon Inspector General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Defense_Office_of_Inspector_General) found that the Biden administration seemed unaware of the pace of weapons deliveries.[^cite_note-570]

Throughout the conflict, Biden consistently refused Ukrainian requests to allow them to utilize weapons against Russian military targets inside [Russia](25391.md). An exception was granted in May 2024 for targets in the vicinity of [Kharkiv](2788558.md) for "counter-fire" purposes.[^cite_note-575] Biden also blocked access for some weapons systems altogether, typically citing fears of escalation, only to permit deliveries for some weapons later on.[^cite_note-581]

#### China affairs

Further information: [China–United States relations § Biden administration (2021–2025)](277880.md#Biden_administration_\(2021%E2%80%932025\))

The [Solomon Islands-China security pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Solomon_Islands_relations) caused alarm in late 2022, as China could build military bases across the South Pacific. Biden sought to strengthen ties with Australia and New Zealand in the wake of the deal.[^cite_note-582][^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584] In a September 2022 interview with *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)*, Biden said that U.S. forces would defend Taiwan in the event of "an unprecedented attack" by the Chinese,[^cite_note-585] which is in contrast to the long-standing U.S. policy of "[strategic ambiguity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_ambiguity)" toward China and Taiwan.[^cite_note-Kine-586][^cite_note-587][^cite_note-588] The September comments came after three previous comments by Biden that the U.S. would defend Taiwan in the event of a Chinese invasion.[^cite_note-theories-589] Amid increasing tension with China, Biden's administration has repeatedly walked back his statements and asserted that U.S. policy toward Taiwan has not changed.[^cite_note-theories-589][^cite_note-590][^cite_note-Kine-586] In late 2022, Biden issued several executive orders and federal rules designed to slow Chinese technological growth, and maintain U.S. leadership over computing, biotech, and clean energy.[^cite_note-591]

On February 4, 2023, Biden ordered the United States Air Force to shoot down a [suspected Chinese surveillance balloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Chinese_balloon_incident) off the coast of [South Carolina](27956.md).[^cite_note-592][^cite_note-593] The [Chinese government](23239.md) denied that the balloon was a surveillance device, instead claiming it was a civilian [airship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airship) that had blown off course.[^cite_note-594][^cite_note-595] Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) postponed his planned visit to China as the incident further damaged U.S.-China relations.[^cite_note-596][^cite_note-597][^cite_note-598] In May 2024, the Biden administration doubled [tariffs](55551.md) on [solar cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_cell) imported from China and more than tripled tariffs on [lithium-ion electric vehicle batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium-ion_battery) imported from China.[^cite_note-erwgs-599] It also raised tariffs on imports of Chinese steel, aluminum, and medical materials.[^cite_note-erwgs-599]

In April 2024, Biden signed the [Protecting Americans from Foreign Adversary Controlled Applications Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protecting_Americans_from_Foreign_Adversary_Controlled_Applications_Act), which would ban [social networking services](2041117.md) if they are determined by the president and relevant provisions to be a "foreign adversary controlled application". The act explicitly applies to [ByteDance Ltd.](54450737.md) and its subsidiaries, which are based in China. It ceases to be applicable if the application is [divested](981983.md) and no longer considered to be controlled by a [foreign adversary of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_foreign_adversaries#Other_meanings). Biden had signed the [No TikTok on Government Devices Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_TikTok_on_Government_Devices_Act) in December 2022, prohibiting the use of TikTok on devices owned by the federal government.[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601][^cite_note-602]

#### Gaza war

Further information: [United States support for Israel in the Gaza war](65284610.md)

In October 2023, Hamas [launched a surprise attack on Israel](74998317.md) that [devolved into an intensified conflict](74998519.md), jeopardizing the administration's push to normalize relations [between Israel and Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_relations).[^cite_note-603] Biden stated [his unequivocal support for Israel](65284610.md) and condemned the attack by Hamas, but discouraged Israel from initiating a ground invasion of Gaza.[^cite_note-604][^cite_note-WarWoodward-605] Upon Israel's retaliation against Hamas, Biden deployed aircraft carriers in the region to deter others from joining the war,[^cite_note-606] and called for an additional \$14 billion in military aid to Israel.[^cite_note-607] He later began pressuring Israel to address the growing [humanitarian crisis in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Israeli_blockade_of_the_Gaza_Strip).[^cite_note-608] Biden rejected calls for a ceasefire but said he supported "humanitarian pauses" to deliver aid to the [Gaza Strip](12047.md).[^cite_note-609] He asked Israel to pause its invasion of Gaza for at least three days to allow for hostage negotiations; Israel agreed to daily four-hour pauses.[^cite_note-610] He also directed the U.S. military to facilitate the delivery of humanitarian aid to Palestinians in Gaza.[^cite_note-611] Biden has said he is a [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist).[^cite_note-612][^cite_note-613] He has faced criticism for his unwavering support for Israel. Officials have urged him to take a harder stance against Israel, criticizing his administration's leniency and support despite the Israeli government's contentious offensive, which has led to significant civilian casualties and [humanitarian crises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_humanitarian_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-614][^cite_note-615][^cite_note-616]

Following the [killing of Palestinian civilians receiving food aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flour_massacre) on February 29, 2024, Biden said the current level of aid flowing into Gaza was insufficient.[^cite_note-617] On March 3, the U.S. military began airdropping food aid into Gaza.[^cite_note-618] Several experts called the U.S. airdrops performative and said they would do little to alleviate the [famine in Gaza](75828824.md).[^cite_note-619]

Biden continued to support Israel during the course of the war despite significant domestic opposition to American involvement in it and subsequent widespread [protests](75347645.md). A March 2024 Gallup poll found that a strong majority of Americans disapproved of Israeli conduct during the war.[^cite_note-Gallup_Inc.-620] Beginning in April 2024, widespread [Gaza war protests emerged on university campuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_pro-Palestinian_protests_on_university_campuses_in_the_United_States_in_2024), denouncing Biden.[^cite_note-621]

On May 31, 2024, Biden announced his support for an Israeli [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) proposal, saying that Hamas was "no longer capable" of another large-scale attack.[^cite_note-622][^cite_note-623][^cite_note-624] The proposal, which was intended to establish a permanent ceasefire, bring about the release of all hostages, and lead to the reconstruction of the [Gaza Strip](12047.md), was supported by Hamas officials after mediation by [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Qatar](8238258.md).[^cite_note-625][^cite_note-626] The Netanyahu administration responded that Israel's goals regarding "the destruction of Hamas military and governing capabilities" had not changed and that conditions would need to be met before it would agree to a ceasefire.[^cite_note-627][^cite_note-628][^cite_note-629] In the first year of the war, it was estimated that the Biden administration had sent Israel at least \$17.9 billion in military aid, a record.[^cite_note-knickmeyer-630] In about the same period, it sent Palestinians \$1.2 billion in humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-ching-voa-631] Biden was reported by journalist [Bob Woodward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Woodward) to have clashed throughout the war with Netanyahu over his war strategy and lack of a postwar plan for Gaza.[^cite_note-WarWoodward-605] In the last week of Biden's presidency, Qatari officials announced that Hamas had accepted the ceasefire deal; Biden hailed the deal.[^cite_note-632][^cite_note-633]

#### NATO enlargement

Following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md), Biden expressed support for expanding [NATO](21133.md) to cover [Sweden](5058739.md) and [Finland](10577.md).[^cite_note-634][^cite_note-635] On August 9, 2022, he signed the instruments of ratification stipulating U.S. support for the two countries' entry into NATO.[^cite_note-636][^cite_note-637] [Finnish ascension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland%E2%80%93NATO_relations) occurred on April 4, 2023, but opposition by [Turkey](11125639.md) and [Hungary](13275.md) to [Swedish entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden%E2%80%93NATO_relations) led to a stalemate.[^cite_note-638] Biden led diplomatic talks resulting in formal Swedish ascension into NATO on March 7, 2024.[^cite_note-639][^cite_note-640] He has also expressed openness to [Ukrainian](31750.md) entry into NATO following the end of the conflict,[^cite_note-641] supporting an expedited timetable in its ascension and the removal of steps such as the [Membership Action Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO#Membership_Action_Plan) typically required for NATO entry.[^cite_note-642][^cite_note-643]

### Investigations

#### Retention of classified documents

Main article: [Joe Biden classified documents incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_classified_documents_incident)

In November 2022, Biden's attorneys found classified documents dating from his vice presidency in a "locked closet" at the [Penn Biden Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Biden_Center).[^cite_note-644][^cite_note-nytbatch1-645] According to the White House, the documents were reported to the [U.S. National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._National_Archives), which recovered them the next day.[^cite_note-nytbatch1-645] On November 14, Attorney General [Merrick Garland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland) appointed [John R. Lausch Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Lausch_Jr.) to conduct an investigation.[^cite_note-646][^cite_note-647] On December 20, a second batch of classified documents was discovered in the garage of Biden's [Wilmington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_Delaware) residence.[^cite_note-648]

On January 12, 2023, Garland appointed [Robert K. Hur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_K._Hur) as special counsel to investigate "possible unauthorized removal and retention of classified documents or other records".[^cite_note-649] On January 20, after a 13-hour [consensual search](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_search) by FBI investigators, six more items with classified markings were recovered from Biden's Wilmington residence.[^cite_note-650] FBI agents searched Biden's home in [Rehoboth Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehoboth_Beach,_Delaware) on February 1 and collected papers from his time as vice president, but did not find any classified information.[^cite_note-651] On February 8, 2024, Hur announced that no charges would be brought against Biden.[^cite_note-652]

#### Business activities

Main article: [United States House Oversight Committee investigation into the Biden family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Oversight_Committee_investigation_into_the_Biden_family)

Further information: [Impeachment inquiry into Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Joe_Biden)



Biden taking a [selfie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selfie) with digital content creators in front of the [Eisenhower Executive Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisenhower_Executive_Office_Building) on October 25, 2022

On January 11, 2023, the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) launched [an investigative committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Oversight_Committee_investigation_into_the_Biden_family) into the foreign business activities of Biden's son, [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden), and brother, [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Biden).[^cite_note-653] The committee's chair, Representative [James Comer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Comer), simultaneously investigated alleged corruption related to the [Hunter Biden laptop controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden_laptop_controversy).[^cite_note-654]

On September 12, House speaker [Kevin McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_McCarthy) initiated a formal impeachment inquiry against Biden, saying that the House investigations "paint a picture of corruption" by Biden and his family.[^cite_note-659] Congressional investigations, including [by the House Oversight committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Oversight_Committee_investigation_into_the_Biden_family), have discovered no evidence of wrongdoing by Biden as of December 2023.[^cite_note-664] On December 13, 2023, the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) voted 221–212 to formalize an [impeachment inquiry into Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Joe_Biden).[^cite_note-665][^cite_note-666][^cite_note-667]

In February 2024, [Alexander Smirnov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Smirnov_\(FBI_informant\)), a former intelligence [informant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informant) who was prominent in the bribery allegations against Biden, was charged with [making false statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Making_false_statements).[^cite_note-668] Smirnov admitted he had publicized a false story given to him by Russian intelligence officials with the goal of damaging Biden's reelection campaign.[^cite_note-669][^cite_note-670][^cite_note-671]

### Age and health concerns

Main article: [Age and health concerns about Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden)

As of 2025, Biden was the oldest sitting president in U.S. history. His cognitive health was perceived to have declined by Republicans and some media figures,[^cite_note-672][^cite_note-cbsjuly1age-673] and privately by some Democrats. Members of Biden's family and White House staffers insulated Biden from scrutiny of his advanced aging and decline in acuity.[^cite_note-674][^cite_note-wsjagedec-675][^cite_note-676]

The media widely covered public concern about Biden's mental acuity after a weak performance in a June 2024 presidential debate,[^cite_note-cbsjuly1age-673] but gave it limited coverage beforehand, in part due to harsh pushback from White House officials.[^cite_note-677] Biden initially deemed himself fit to serve two terms as president,[^cite_note-678] but after the 2024 election said that he may have been too old to serve a second term.[^cite_note-679]

As part of the [investigation into Biden's handling of classified documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_classified_documents_incident), special counsel [Robert Hur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hur) said that Biden did not remember when he was vice president ("if it was 2013—when did I stop being vice president?") or when his son Beau died.[^cite_note-680] Hur wrote that his memory "appeared to have significant limitations".[^cite_note-681]

On July 21, 2022, Biden tested positive for [COVID-19](63030231.md) with reportedly mild symptoms.[^cite_note-682][^cite_note-AP_News-683] According to the White House, he was treated with [Paxlovid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paxlovid).[^cite_note-AP_News-683][^cite_note-684] He worked in isolation in the White House for five days[^cite_note-685] and returned to isolation when he tested positive again on July 30.[^cite_note-686][^cite_note-687] On July 17, 2024, Biden again tested positive for COVID-19.[^cite_note-688][^cite_note-689]

## 2024 presidential campaign

Main article: [Joe Biden 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign)

Further information: [2024 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), [Abandon Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abandon_Biden), [Uncommitted National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncommitted_National_Movement), [2024 Joe Biden–Donald Trump presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate), and [Withdrawal of Joe Biden from the 2024 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election)

Biden addressing the nation on July 24, 2024, about his withdrawal from the election three days earlier

Ending months of speculation,[^cite_note-690][^cite_note-691] on April 25, 2023, Biden confirmed he would run for reelection as president in the [2024 election](59568546.md), with Harris again as his running mate.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-692] On the day of his announcement, a Gallup poll found that Biden's approval rating was 37 percent, with most of those surveyed saying the economy was their biggest concern.[^cite_note-pollrui-693] During his campaign, Biden promoted [higher economic growth and recovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Joe_Biden_administration).[^cite_note-694][^cite_note-695] He frequently stated his intention to "finish the job" as a political rallying cry.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-692][^cite_note-696][^cite_note-697]

[U.S. Representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Representative) [Dean Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Phillips) ran against Biden in the 2024 Democratic presidential primaries.[^cite_note-698] Phillips [campaigned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Phillips_2024_presidential_campaign) as a younger alternative to Biden,[^cite_note-699] arguing that he would be a stronger opponent to Trump in the general election.[^cite_note-700][^cite_note-701] Biden was not on the ballot in the January 23, [New Hampshire primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_New_Hampshire_Democratic_presidential_primary), but won it in a write-in campaign with 63.8% of the vote.[^cite_note-702] He had wanted South Carolina to be the first primary, and [won that state on February 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_South_Carolina_Democratic_presidential_primary) with 96.2% of the vote.[^cite_note-703] Biden received 89.3% of the vote in [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Nevada_Democratic_presidential_primary) and 81.1% of the vote in [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Michigan_Democratic_presidential_primary). On March 5 ("Super Tuesday"), he won 15 of 16 primaries, netting 80% or more of the vote in 13.[^cite_note-704][^cite_note-705] Biden lost the [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_American_Samoa_Democratic_presidential_caucuses) contest to venture capitalist [Jason Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Palmer_\(politician\)), becoming the first incumbent president to lose a contest while appearing on the ballot since [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) in [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-706] On March 6, Phillips suspended his campaign and endorsed Biden.[^cite_note-707] On March 12, Biden reached more than the 1,968 delegates needed to win the Democratic nomination, becoming the presumptive nominee.[^cite_note-708][^cite_note-709][^cite_note-710]

The [first presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate) was held on June 27, 2024, between Biden and Trump. Biden's performance was widely criticized, with commentators saying he frequently lost his train of thought and gave meandering answers.[^cite_note-reuters-biden-debate-711][^cite_note-apjune27-712][^cite_note-PoliticoJune28Dems-713] Several newspaper columnists declared Trump the winner,[^cite_note-718] and polling indicated the majority of viewers believed Trump won.[^cite_note-719] After the debate raised questions about [his health and age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_of_Joe_Biden), Biden faced calls to withdraw from the race, including from [Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Democrats_who_opposed_the_Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign)[^cite_note-nbc-biden-step-down-720] and the [editorial boards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Editorial_boards) of various major news outlets.[^cite_note-\:0-721][^cite_note-722]

Biden initially insisted that he would remain a candidate,[^cite_note-723] but on July 21, he withdrew from the race, writing that this was "in the best interest of my party and the country".[^cite_note-724] He immediately endorsed Harris to replace him as the party's presidential nominee.[^cite_note-725][^cite_note-726] On August 6, 2024, Harris officially became the Democratic presidential nominee.[^cite_note-727] This was the first time an eligible incumbent had withdrawn a bid for reelection since [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-728]

In the general election, Trump defeated Harris. The Senate [went Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_Senate_elections) for the first time since 2018. In a nationally televised speech after the election, Biden congratulated Trump and promised a "peaceful and orderly" transition of power.[^cite_note-729] In a January 2025 interview, Biden claimed he could have defeated Trump had he not been persuaded to withdraw from the election, despite lagging behind Trump in polling.[^cite_note-730][^cite_note-731] A [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll conducted on November 6–7, 2024, found that if Biden had been the Democratic nominee, Trump would have won the popular vote by 49% to 42%. Trump won the popular vote over Harris by 49.8% to 48.3%.[^cite_note-732]

## Post-presidency (2025–present)



Biden (front row, fifth from the right), on his 83rd birthday, attending the [funeral of Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney#Death_and_funeral) in November 2025

Biden's term ended on January 20, 2025.[^cite_note-733] At the end of his presidency, Biden designated former [senior advisors](29024072.md) [Anthony Bernal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Bernal) and [Annie Tomasini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Tomasini) to raise funds for the [Joseph R. Biden Jr. Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_R._Biden_Jr._Presidential_Library).[^cite_note-734] He later signed with talent agency [Creative Artists Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Artists_Agency) (CAA), which previously represented him from 2017 to 2020.[^cite_note-735][^cite_note-736] On February 7, President Trump revoked Biden's [security clearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_clearance).[^cite_note-737] Former presidents have traditionally been granted access to intelligence briefings, though such access is at the sitting president's discretion. In 2021, Biden had revoked Trump's security clearance for his role in inciting the [January 6 Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_Capitol_attack).[^cite_note-738][^cite_note-739] On April 15, Biden made his first major public appearance since leaving office, delivering remarks at an Advocates, Counselors and Representatives for the Disabled (ACRD) conference in [Chicago](6886.md).[^cite_note-740][^cite_note-741] On April 26, Biden and his wife attended the state funeral of [Pope Francis](1687680.md) in Vatican City.[^cite_note-742] On May 7, Biden appeared on *[The View](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_View)* to defend his presidential legacy.[^cite_note-743] On September 6, Biden announced that his proposed presidential center would be in Delaware.[^cite_note-744] On November 20, Biden and his wife attended former Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney)'s funeral.[^cite_note-745]

On March 6, 2026, Biden gave a speech at civil rights activist [Jesse Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Jackson)'s memorial service.[^cite_note-746] On April 13, he returned to Syracuse University for a ceremony unveiling his official portrait at the College of Law.[^cite_note-747] On June 18, Biden attended the [Barack Obama Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_Presidential_Center)'s opening ceremony.[^cite_note-748]

In July 2026, Biden announced his third memoir, *[Promise Me, America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promise_Me,_America)*, which focuses on his presidency. It is scheduled for release on November 17, 2026.[^cite_note-749][^cite_note-750]

### Health

On May 18, 2025, Biden's office announced that he had been diagnosed with aggressive [prostate cancer](88078.md) with [bone metastasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_metastasis) during a routine physical examination.[^cite_note-751] On May 30, Biden confirmed that he had begun treatment.[^cite_note-752] On September 4, Biden's spokesperson confirmed that he had recently undergone [Mohs surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohs_surgery) to remove skin cancer lesions from his forehead. The procedure followed a 2023 removal of a [basal-cell carcinoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basal-cell_carcinoma) from his chest.[^cite_note-753] On October 11, Biden's spokesperson confirmed that he was undergoing [radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiation_therapy) and [hormone therapies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormonal_therapy_\(oncology\)) for his prostate cancer.[^cite_note-754] On October 20, Biden completed a course of radiation therapy at Penn Medicine Radiation Oncology at the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania).[^cite_note-bidenhealth-755][^cite_note-756]

In August 2026, Hunter Biden said the cancer had "spread, metastasized into his bones and further" and was "very painful and it's very debilitating in many respects".[^cite_note-757][^cite_note-758] In September 2026, Biden announced that the radiation treatment he received for prostate cancer last fall "worked as intended" and that doctors helped in disease control.[^cite_note-759]

## Political positions

Main article: [Political positions of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Joe_Biden)

As a senator, Biden was regarded as a [moderate Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate_Democrat).[^cite_note-760] As a presidential nominee, Biden's platform had been called the most progressive of any major party platform in history, although not within his party's ideological vanguard.[^cite_note-761] Biden says his positions are deeply influenced by [Catholic social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_social_teaching).[^cite_note-762][^cite_note-763][^cite_note-764]

According to political scientist Carlo Invernizzi Accetti, Biden represents an Americanized form of [Christian democracy](328969.md), taking positions characteristic of both the [center-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center-right) and [center-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center-left).[^cite_note-Foreign_Policy-765] Biden has cited the Catholic philosopher [Jacques Maritain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Maritain), credited with starting the Christian democratic movement, as immensely influential in his thinking.[^cite_note-766] Other analysts have likened his ideology to traditional [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States), "a doctrine of liberty, equality, justice and individual rights that relies... on a strong federal government for enforcement".[^cite_note-Greenberg-767] In 2022, journalist [Sasha Issenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasha_Issenberg) wrote that Biden's "most valuable political skill" was "an innate compass for the ever-shifting mainstream of the Democratic Party".[^cite_note-768] Republican critics claimed Biden's climate policy was [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism).[^cite_note-769][^cite_note-770][^cite_note-771]

Biden proposed partially reversing the corporate tax cuts of the [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act_of_2017).[^cite_note-772][^cite_note-773] He voted for the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA)[^cite_note-774] and the [Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership).[^cite_note-775] Biden is a staunch supporter of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) (ACA).[^cite_note-ACA203-776][^cite_note-777] He promoted a plan to build upon it,[^cite_note-ACA203-776] aiming to expand [health insurance coverage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_coverage_in_the_United_States) to 97% of Americans, including by creating a [public health insurance option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_insurance_option).[^cite_note-778]

Biden did not support national [same-sex marriage](92656.md) rights while in the Senate and voted for the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act),[^cite_note-779] but opposed proposals for constitutional amendments that would have banned same-sex marriage nationwide.[^cite_note-780] Biden has supported same-sex marriage since 2012.[^cite_note-NYT_Biden_Evolution_on_LGBTQ-74][^cite_note-781] As a senator, Biden forged deep relationships with police groups and was a chief proponent of a [Law Enforcement Officers' Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_Enforcement_Officers'_Bill_of_Rights) measure that police unions supported but police chiefs opposed.[^cite_note-782][^cite_note-783] In 2020, Biden also ran on decriminalizing [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis),[^cite_note-784] after advocating harsher penalties for drug use as a senator.[^cite_note-785][^cite_note-786]

Biden believes action must be taken on [climate change](5042951.md). As a senator, he co-sponsored the [Boxer–Sanders Global Warming Pollution Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Warming_Pollution_Reduction_Act_of_2007), the most stringent climate bill in the [United States Senate](24909346.md).[^cite_note-787] Biden supports [nature conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_conservation). According to a report from the [Center for American Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_American_Progress), he broke several records in this domain.[^cite_note-788] He took steps to protect [old-growth forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest).[^cite_note-789] Biden opposes drilling for oil in the [Arctic National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_National_Wildlife_Refuge).[^cite_note-790] He wants to achieve a carbon-free power sector in the U.S. by 2035 and stop emissions completely by 2050.[^cite_note-791] His program included reentering the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md), [green building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_building) and more.[^cite_note-792] Biden supports [environmental justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_justice), including [climate justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_justice) and [ocean justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_justice).[^cite_note-797] Biden called global temperature rise above the 1.5°C limit the "only existential threat humanity faces even more frightening than a nuclear war".[^cite_note-798] Despite his clean energy policies and congressional Republicans characterizing them as a "War on American Energy", domestic oil production reached a record high in October 2023.[^cite_note-799]

Biden has said the U.S. needs to "get tough" on China, calling it the "most serious competitor" that poses challenges to the United States' "prosperity, security, and democratic values".[^cite_note-800][^cite_note-801] Biden has spoken about human rights abuses in the [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) region to Chinese leader [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), pledging to sanction and commercially restrict Chinese government officials and entities who carry out repression.[^cite_note-802][^cite_note-803] Biden has said he is against [regime change](154074.md) but is for providing non-military support to opposition movements.[^cite_note-804] He opposed direct U.S. [intervention in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya),[^cite_note-805][^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2-185] voted against U.S. participation in the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War),[^cite_note-806] voted in favor of the [Iraq War](5043324.md),[^cite_note-807] and supports a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution) in the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md).[^cite_note-808] Biden pledged to end U.S. support for the [Saudi Arabian-led intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) and to reevaluate the [United States' relationship](9753135.md) with [Saudi Arabia](349303.md).[^cite_note-foreign-policy2-242] Biden supported extending the [New START](26709878.md) arms control treaty with Russia to limit the number of [nuclear weapons](21785.md) deployed by both sides.[^cite_note-809][^cite_note-810] In 2021, Biden officially [recognized](12001341.md) the [Armenian genocide](140376.md), becoming the first U.S. president to do so.[^cite_note-811][^cite_note-814]

Biden supported [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements) throughout his presidency, though he personally opposes abortion because of his Catholic faith.[^cite_note-815][^cite_note-816] In 2019, he said he supported *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* and repealing the [Hyde Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyde_Amendment).[^cite_note-817][^cite_note-818] After *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)*, he criticized [near-total bans on abortion access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_law_in_the_United_States_by_state) passed in a majority of [Republican](32070.md)-controlled states,[^cite_note-819] and took measures to protect [abortion rights in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-820] Biden rejected calls to provide abortion services on [federal land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_lands).[^cite_note-821][^cite_note-822] He vowed to sign a bill codifying the protections of *Roe* into federal law; such a bill passed the House in 2022, but was unable to clear the Senate filibuster.[^cite_note-823][^cite_note-824]

## Family

Main article: [Family of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_of_Joe_Biden)

Photo of Biden and his wife smiling, dressed casually

Biden and his second wife, [Jill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Biden), met in 1975 and married in 1977.

Biden married [Neilia Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neilia_Hunter_Biden), a student at [Syracuse University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_University), on August 27, 1966,[^cite_note-ap-timeline-25][^cite_note-825] after overcoming her parents' disinclination for her to wed a Catholic. Their wedding was held in a Catholic church in [Skaneateles, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skaneateles,_New_York_\(village\)).[^cite_note-826] They had three children: [Joseph R. "Beau" Biden III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beau_Biden), [Robert Hunter Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden), and Naomi Christina "Amy" Biden.[^cite_note-ap-timeline-25]

A few weeks after Biden was elected senator, his wife Neilia and one-year-old daughter Naomi were killed in an automobile accident in [Hockessin, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hockessin,_Delaware), on December 18, 1972.[^cite_note-ap-timeline-25][^cite_note-827] Their sons [Beau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beau_Biden) (aged 3) and [Hunter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden) (aged 2) were in the car and were taken to hospital with non-life-threatening injuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitcover201093,_98-828] He considered resigning to care for them,[^cite_note-aap08-bio-18] but [Senate Majority Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Majority_Leader) [Mike Mansfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Mansfield) persuaded him not to.[^cite_note-829] Biden contemplated suicide and was filled with anger and religious doubt.[^cite_note-830][^cite_note-831] He wrote that he "felt God had played a horrible trick" on him[^cite_note-832] and had trouble focusing on work.[^cite_note-833][^cite_note-834]

Biden met teacher [Jill Tracy Jacobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Tracy_Jacobs) in 1975 on a [blind date](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_date).[^cite_note-835] They married at the [United Nations chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Center_for_the_United_Nations) in New York on June 17, 1977,[^cite_note-836][^cite_note-837] and spent their honeymoon at [Lake Balaton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Balaton) in the [Hungarian People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_People's_Republic).[^cite_note-838][^cite_note-839] Biden credits her with the renewal of his interest in politics and life.[^cite_note-840]

In 1981, the couple had a daughter, [Ashley Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashley_Biden),[^cite_note-ap-timeline-25] who is a social worker, activist, and fashion designer.[^cite_note-841] Jill helped raise her stepsons, Hunter and Beau, who were seven and eight respectively at the time of her marriage. Hunter has worked as a Washington lobbyist and investment adviser; his business dealings, [personal life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden_laptop_controversy), and [legal troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weiss_special_counsel_investigation) came under [significant scrutiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Oversight_Committee_investigation_into_the_Biden_family) during his father's presidency. In December 2024, Biden pardoned Hunter following his conviction on gun and tax charges despite repeated promises that he would not do so.[^cite_note-846] Beau became an Army [judge-advocate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge-advocate) in Iraq and later [Delaware attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_attorney_general)[^cite_note-847] before dying of brain cancer in 2015.[^cite_note-848][^cite_note-849]

## Public image

Main article: [Public image of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_image_of_Joe_Biden)



A [political cartoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_cartoon) mocking Biden's 2024 presidential campaign, which he ultimately withdrew following concerns about his age and health

Biden was consistently ranked one of the least wealthy [members of the Senate](24909346.md),[^cite_note-850][^cite_note-851] which he attributed to having been elected young.[^cite_note-852] Feeling that less-wealthy public officials may be tempted to accept contributions in exchange for political favors, he proposed [campaign finance reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_finance_reform_in_the_United_States) measures during his first term.[^cite_note-cby-44-64] While a senator, Biden was viewed as being close to the credit card company [MBNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MBNA), a major contributor to his campaigns since 1989, sometimes being called the "senator from MBNA".[^cite_note-853] As of November 2009, Biden's net worth was \$27,012.[^cite_note-854] By November 2020, the Bidens were worth \$9 million, largely due to sales of Biden's books and speaking fees after his vice presidency.[^cite_note-855][^cite_note-856]

Political columnist [David S. Broder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_S._Broder) wrote that Biden has grown over time: "He responds to real people—that's been consistent throughout. And his ability to understand himself and deal with other politicians has gotten much, much better."[^cite_note-watn020109-35] Journalist [James Traub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Traub) has written that "Biden is the kind of fundamentally happy person who can be as generous toward others as he is to himself".[^cite_note-nytm-traub-105] Particularly since the 2015 death of his elder son Beau, Biden has been noted for his empathetic nature and ability to communicate about grief.[^cite_note-857][^cite_note-858]

Journalist and TV anchor [Wolf Blitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_Blitzer) has called Biden loquacious;[^cite_note-859] journalist Mark Bowden has said that he is famous for "talking too much", leaning in close "like an old pal with something urgent to tell you".[^cite_note-The_Atlantic-269] He often deviates from prepared remarks,[^cite_note-860] and sometimes "puts his foot in his mouth".[^cite_note-nyt-no2-142][^cite_note-861][^cite_note-862] Biden has a reputation for being prone to [gaffes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_gaffe).[^cite_note-863][^cite_note-864][^cite_note-865] *The New York Times* wrote that Biden's "weak filters make him capable of blurting out pretty much anything".[^cite_note-nyt-no2-142]

According to *The New York Times*, Biden often embellishes elements of his life or exaggerates, a trait also noted by *[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)* in 2014.[^cite_note-The_New_Yorker-866][^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-867] For instance, he has claimed to have been more active in the [civil rights movement](49001.md) than he actually was, and has falsely recalled being an excellent student who earned three college degrees.[^cite_note-The_New_Yorker-866] The *Times* wrote, "Mr. Biden's folksiness can veer into folklore, with dates that don't quite add up and details that are exaggerated or wrong, the factual edges shaved off to make them more powerful for audiences."[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-867]

### Job approval

See also: [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration), and [2024–2025 opinion polling on the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_opinion_polling_on_the_Biden_administration)

According to [Morning Consult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morning_Consult) polling, Biden maintained an [approval rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_poll) above 50% during his presidency's first eight months. In August 2021, it began to decline, reaching the low forties by December.[^cite_note-868] This was attributed to the Afghanistan withdrawal, increasing hospitalizations from the [Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant), [high inflation and gas prices](69605317.md), disarray within the Democratic Party, and a general decline in popularity customary in politics.[^cite_note-873] In 2023, Biden's approval rating was the lowest of any modern[^cite_note-874] U.S. president after three years in office.[^cite_note-gallup2023end-875]

[Gallup, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) found Biden's approval ratings to be consistently above 50% during his first few months in office,[^cite_note-Gallup_Approval-876][^cite_note-877] but by August, his ratings began to decline.[^cite_note-878] He had a 98% approval rating from Democrats in February 2021, but by December only 78% approved of his presidency.[^cite_note-BidenGallup-879][^cite_note-Gallup_Approval-876] By October 2023, his rating among Democrats had reached a record low of 75%.[^cite_note-gallup2023end-875][^cite_note-880] His approval rating among Republicans has been consistently in the single digits, aside from his first few months in office.[^cite_note-BidenGallup-879]

Gallup also noted that Biden's public support eroded each year he was in office: he averaged 49% approval in his first year,[^cite_note-881] 41% in his second,[^cite_note-882] 40% in his third,[^cite_note-883] and 39% in 2024.[^cite_note-gallup20250117-884] In July 2024, just before he withdrew from the presidential election, Gallup found his approval rating had fallen to an all-time low of 36%.[^cite_note-885] Gallup found that Biden had an average approval rating of 42.2% throughout his presidency, lower than all other presidents' except Trump's in his first term, at 41.1%. Biden's approval rating in January 2025 was 40%, which was low but still higher than several other presidents' final approval ratings.[^cite_note-gallup20250117-884] Gallup's averaged polls of Biden's presidency found that he was the second-least popular president in its polling history, after Trump.[^cite_note-gallup20250117-884]
